    Onboarding guide

    28. Common Cloud Provider Adapters (AWS, Azure, GCP)

    This chapter covers the cloud vendor adapter packages, MMCA.Common.Aws, MMCA.Common.Azure and MMCA.Common.Gcp, plus MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup, which holds the three builder-level Azure calls (Key Vault configuration, the Azure Monitor exporter and the DataProtection key ring) with no persistence or messaging dependency. MMCA.Common.Azure references it and type-forwards those three classes, so a host that references MMCA.Common.Azure still sees them (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/TypeForwards.cs:6-8). The rule they exist to keep is short: the core packages reference no cloud SDK. Every vendor integration lives in an adapter package that references the core, never the reverse, and a host opts in with one registration call per capability (ADR-134). So the core defines an abstraction (or an extension point) and ships an inert default. Each adapter type implements that abstraction against one vendor SDK, and all a host has to decide is which package to reference and which calls to make. [Rubric §3, Clean Architecture] asks whether dependencies point inward toward policy and away from infrastructure detail. Here the vendor SDKs sit at the outermost ring, and nothing in Domain, Application or Infrastructure names them. [Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain] asks whether a consumer pays only for the dependencies it uses. A host that never calls an AWS adapter never restores an AWS package.

    The abstractions the adapters implement. Six extension points in the core take an adapter. Their contracts are taught where they are declared, and this chapter only implements them:

    • File storage: IFileStorageService (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Storage/IFileStorageService.cs:15, ADR-045). The core default is NullFileStorageService (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Storage/NullFileStorageService.cs:14).
    • Native push: the pair INativePushSender (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Notifications/INativePushSender.cs:12) and IPushDeviceRegistrar (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Notifications/IPushDeviceRegistrar.cs:12) (ADR-044). The core defaults are NullNativePushSender and NullPushDeviceRegistrar (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Notifications/Push/NullNativePushSender.cs:12, NullPushDeviceRegistrar.cs:14).
    • Broker transport: IBrokerTransport (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Transports/IBrokerTransport.cs:22), registered through AddBrokerTransport (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:174) and selected by name (ADR-066, ADR-118). The retry, redelivery and backpressure rules every transport shares live once in BrokerTransportPolicies.
    • PostgreSQL connection customization: INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator.cs:23), added through AddNpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Persistence.cs:24).
    • Redis connection customization: IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Caching/IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator.cs:30), added through AddRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Caching.cs:253).
    • Host-level ASP.NET Core extension points: the configuration system (IConfigurationSource), the DataProtection key ring (IXmlRepository, IXmlEncryptor, IXmlDecryptor), and the OpenTelemetry pipeline that AddServiceDefaults() configures. These are framework types, not MMCA ones, so the adapters plug into them directly.

    Per-vendor membership. The 119 types fall into one row per capability. A blank cell means the vendor package has no adapter for that capability.

    Capability (core hook) MMCA.Common.Aws (43) MMCA.Common.Azure + .Azure.Startup (41) MMCA.Common.Gcp (35)
    Registration entry point AwsServiceCollectionExtensions AzureServiceCollectionExtensions GcpServiceCollectionExtensions

    Shared credential plumbing AwsClientEndpoint, AwsStaticCredentials AzureTokenCredentials

    The rows are uneven for real reasons, not because a port is unfinished. GCP ships no broker transport. Its Cloud SQL configurator instead covers the built-in PostgreSQL broker alongside the database contexts (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:72-74). Azure has two database paths: Entra ID for SQL Server, which arrives through the SqlClient extension the package carries with no registration call (ADR-134, Decision; restated at MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:172-173), and Entra token login for Azure Database for PostgreSQL through AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:181). Only Azure ships a Redis configurator (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:230-253). The two broker namespaces are the largest because each transport is framework code on the bare vendor SDK: AzureServiceBusTransport runs on Azure.Messaging.ServiceBus alone and AmazonSqsTransport on the AWS SDK SQS and SNS clients alone (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:13-15, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:14-16), with no third-party messaging library between the framework and the broker (ADR-118). The Azure notifications namespace is large for the same reason: the adapter reaches the hub through the framework's own REST client, NotificationHubRestClient behind INotificationHubRestClient, a typed factory HttpClient authorized with a Shared Access Signature from the connection string, with its own retry (three retries with exponential backoff, or the hub's Retry-After) and a 60-second attempt timeout, so the standard resilience handler is taken off it rather than stacked on top (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:84-90, :110-132; MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRetryPolicy.cs:26, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:43). The GCP notifications namespace keeps its own registry and client interfaces because FCM tokens carry no owner (IFcmInstallationRegistry, IFcmMessagingClient), which puts Firestore and FirebaseAdmin behind something testable (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FirestoreInstallationRegistry.cs:16, FirebaseMessagingClient.cs:17).

    How the swap works at runtime: inert core defaults, then an adapter override. AddInfrastructure registers every core default with TryAdd: services.TryAddTransient<INativePushSender, NullNativePushSender>() and its registrar sibling (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:377-378), and services.TryAddTransient<IFileStorageService, NullFileStorageService>() (DependencyInjection.cs:385). The adapters register with a plain Add: services.AddTransient<IFileStorageService, AzureBlobFileStorageService>() (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:71), S3FileStorageService (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:68), GcsFileStorageService (GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:63), and the push pairs (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:133-134, AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:194-195, GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:166-167). The adapter therefore wins whichever side runs first. Called before AddInfrastructure, it makes the core TryAdd skip. Called after, it is the last registration, which is the one a single-service resolve returns. The vendor SDK clients themselves go in with TryAddSingleton (for example the BlobContainerClient at AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:64-70, S3StorageClientFactory.Create at AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:67, the SNS and DynamoDB clients at AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:191-192, and the Firestore and FCM clients at GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:163-165). A host that registered its own client first, an emulator endpoint for example, keeps it. [Rubric §2, Design Patterns] asks whether patterns solve a real problem. This is the Adapter pattern with a Null Object default: application code depends on one interface, and swapping the cloud is a registration change.

    How a host selects a vendor: reference, call, then configuration as the gate. Every adapter call is unconditional in the host and a no-op until its configuration is complete. The class summaries say so in identical words (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:20-26, AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:20-25, GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:17-22), and the bodies enforce it with early returns. Azure Blob needs a ContainerName plus an absolute ServiceUri or a ConnectionString (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:52-62). S3 needs a Bucket (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:59-62). GCS needs a GcsBucket (GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:57-60). Push needs NativePush:Enabled plus the vendor's sub-section (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:103-108, AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:180-185, GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:155-160). The three storage adapters read the same FileStorage section, each through its own gate key, so a section that carries only the S3 Bucket or the Azure ContainerName leaves the GCS call inert (GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:33-41). The four token-login calls are gated on a boolean Enabled each: Rds:IamAuthentication, CloudSql:IamAuthentication, AzurePostgreSql:EntraAuthentication and AzureRedis:EntraAuthentication (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:112-116, GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:95-99, AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:185-191, :234-240). The builder-level calls follow the same posture with a single gate key each: KeyVault:Uri (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:82), SecretsManager:NamePrefix (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:82), DataProtection:BlobStorageUri, S3ObjectUri or GcsObjectUri (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:56, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:23, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:24), APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureMonitorExporterExtensions.cs:18), AwsTelemetry:Region (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:105) and GoogleCloudTelemetry:ProjectId (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:23). The result is that local development and tests take no cloud dependency at startup, and a deployment switches a capability on through configuration alone. The secrets sources are deliberately not called from AddServiceDefaults() for exactly that reason (AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:59). They also use one naming convention (a double dash maps to the configuration separator, so ConnectionStrings--Default arrives as ConnectionStrings:Default), which lets a secret set move between Key Vault and Secrets Manager by renaming its prefix alone. Both points are recorded on AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:80 and AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:82.

    Broker transports are selected by name, not by type. AddBrokerMessaging picks the bus from the string MessageBus:Provider. RabbitMq, PostgreSql and InProcess are built in, and any other name must match an IBrokerTransport an adapter registered earlier. A name with no registered transport throws, listing the known names and naming the call to add (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:26-37, the throw at :225-230). The two adapter calls are therefore one-liners that hand a transport instance to AddBrokerTransport: AddAzureServiceBusTransport() (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:154-155) and AddAmazonSqsTransport() (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:86-87). Each transport reports its provider name, AzureServiceBus or AmazonSqs, through Name (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:40, :59; AmazonSqsTransport.cs:43, :62). The ordering is load-bearing: the adapter call must come BEFORE AddBrokerMessaging, which picks the transport while it registers the bus (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:140-142, AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:74-76). Inside, the two transports have the same shape: a connection (AzureServiceBusConnection, AmazonSqsConnection), entity naming (AzureServiceBusEntityNames, AmazonSqsEntityNames), a topology provisioner (AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner, AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner), a publisher, a receiver, a delivery handler, a header mapping and a settlement interface (IAzureServiceBusSettlement, IAmazonSqsSettlement). With MessageBus:ProvisionTopology on (the default) the receiver creates the topics, the host's queue and its subscriptions idempotently when it starts, and the publisher creates a missing topic on its first publish (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:17-21, AmazonSqsTransport.cs:18-24). Vendor limits stay inside the transport. SQS caps a message delay at 15 minutes (MaxDelaySeconds = 900, AmazonSqsTransport.cs:46), so the framework receive pipeline shortens every longer second-level redelivery interval to it (AmazonSqsTransport.cs:28-30), and every header travels packed in one message attribute so the 10-attribute cap never applies (AmazonSqsTransport.cs:24-25). Service Bus schedules a redelivery arbitrarily far ahead and dead-letters to the queue's native dead-letter queue (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:22-24). The Service Bus transport signs in with a Microsoft Entra token when MessageBus:AzureServiceBus:FullyQualifiedNamespace is set, so the namespace can run with shared access keys switched off, and uses the broker connection string otherwise. A connection string carrying UseDevelopmentEmulator=true targets the local emulator through ServiceBusEmulatorSupport and needs MessageBus:EmulatorAdminEndpoint (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:27-34). [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] asks whether transport choice is isolated from message semantics. Here the semantics live in the core and its shared policies, and the adapter supplies only the wire.

    Identity instead of stored credentials. Each adapter defaults to the platform's ambient identity and treats a static key as the exception. Azure uses DefaultAzureCredential for Blob, Key Vault and the DataProtection blob (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:67, AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:117, AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:77), and AzureTokenCredentials builds the same credential pinned to a user-assigned managed identity when a client id is configured, for Redis and Service Bus (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureTokenCredentials.cs:12, :21-23). GCP uses Application Default Credentials and has no key-file or HMAC setting at all (GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:38-39, :133-135). AWS falls back to the default credential chain unless AccessKeyId and SecretAccessKey are both set. AwsStaticCredentials enforces the both-or-neither rule at registration rather than on the first call (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:64-65, :118-121, :187-188). The three PostgreSQL configurators push the same idea down to the database connection. RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator, CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator and AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator all implement INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Rds/RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:20-21, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23). Each replaces the password with a short-lived token: an RDS token refreshed every 10 minutes, a Cloud SQL OAuth2 token refreshed every minute, or an Entra token refreshed every four minutes, retried 30 seconds after a failure in every case (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:90-93, GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:69-73, AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:26-29). Because that token travels as the password, all three registration calls refuse any configured PostgreSQL connection string that does not require TLS, and they refuse it at startup, not on the first query (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:123-125, GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:101-103, AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:193-195). AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator does the same for every framework Redis connection (the client and distributed cache, the output-cache store, the SignalR backplane and the health check), and a Redis connection string that still carries an access key or sets ssl=false fails when its connection is configured (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:21, AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:210-226). [Rubric §11, Security] asks whether secrets are kept out of code and configuration and whether credentials are short-lived. These adapters are where the framework answers that question for each cloud.

    Scale-out and observability concerns at the host level. The three DataProtection calls fix one specific multi-replica failure. The framework default keeps the key ring in memory, so a cookie or antiforgery token minted by one replica cannot be decrypted by another. Persisting the ring to one shared store (an Azure blob, one S3 object, or one Cloud Storage object) gives every replica the same keys (the class summaries above AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:20, AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:21 and GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:22). AWS and GCP build their own repositories. S3XmlRepository implements IXmlRepository over one S3 object, rewritten read-modify-write under an S3 conditional write (If-Match on the ETag it read) so two replicas adding a key at once cannot overwrite each other, and asks for SSE-KMS under a configured key (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3XmlRepository.cs:12-28, class at :36). AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions plugs it in through PersistKeysToAmazonS3 (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:95). GCP builds its own repository and KMS encryptor and decryptor: GcsXmlRepository implements IXmlRepository, and GcpKmsXmlEncryptor and GcpKmsXmlDecryptor implement the framework's XML encryption interfaces (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcsXmlRepository.cs:31, GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:25, GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:16). It rewrites the single object under a generation precondition so two replicas cannot overwrite each other's new key (documented on GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:77). Azure and GCP protect the ring with an at-rest key when one is configured (DataProtection:KeyVaultKeyUri, DataProtection:GcpKmsKeyName), and otherwise register a hosted service that logs one startup Warning, AzureKeyRingAtRestNoticeService and GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService (AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:90-98, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:6-13, GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:114-120, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:14-18). The three telemetry calls add a vendor exporter beside the vendor-neutral OTLP exporter that AddServiceDefaults() configures, and all three must run AFTER it so the service-defaults filter processors see spans first. Each enforces that order even when its gate key is unset, so a misordered host fails on a developer machine (AzureMonitorExporterExtensions.cs:42-51, AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:89-99, GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:101-110). The Google and AWS exporters send traces and logs only, and metrics go through a collector, because one request is capped at 200 data points on the Google Telemetry API and at 1,000 on AWS (GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:78-84, AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:66-69). The AWS exporter writes OTLP to X-Ray and CloudWatch Logs with every request signed by Signature Version 4 in AwsSigV4ExportHandler, and exports logs only when AwsTelemetry:LogGroup and AwsTelemetry:LogStream are both set (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:39-51, :252). Neither exporter fetches a credential at registration. The Google token is fetched by the hosted service GoogleCloudTelemetryAccessToken at host start (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Telemetry/GoogleCloudTelemetryAccessToken.cs:28-30). The AWS default chain is warmed at host start by AwsTelemetryCredentialWarmup, where a failure is one Warning rather than a failed host, and static keys take StaticAwsTelemetryCredentialSource instead of DefaultChainAwsTelemetryCredentialSource, both behind IAwsTelemetryCredentialSource (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:57-62, :124-136). [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] asks whether telemetry reaches an operator without coupling code to a backend. Here the instrumentation stays vendor-neutral and only the exporter is chosen per cloud.

    Guardrails against a silently missing adapter. A quiet default is right for a host that never switched a capability on and wrong for one that did. Two startup tripwires close that gap. In the core, the internal CloudAdapterTripwire options validator, wired with ValidateOnStart in AddInfrastructure (DependencyInjection.cs:243-246, class at MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Configuration/CloudAdapterTripwire.cs:32), fails startup when NativePush:Enabled is true but the resolved INativePushSender is still NullNativePushSender, with a message naming all three push adapter calls and their keys (CloudAdapterTripwire.cs:77-82). File storage is deliberately unchecked. A FileStorage section is routinely shared across every service of a solution, so it does not declare intent, and a host that does store files without an adapter is not silent anyway, because NullFileStorageService fails every upload with FileStorage.NotConfigured (CloudAdapterTripwire.cs:24-28). In the Aspire package, HostIntegrationTripwire fails startup when configuration asks for a vendor integration whose adapter call left no HostIntegrationMarker receipt: KeyVault:Uri, APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING, the three DataProtection object keys, both secrets prefixes, GoogleCloudTelemetry:ProjectId, AwsTelemetry:Region and the boolean token-login gates (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:15, keys from :19). The adapter calls leave that receipt only past their gate (for example AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:129-138, AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:197-205, AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:120-122), and a host still carrying the retired DataProtection:SsmParameterPrefix key fails the same way, naming the S3 key that replaced it (HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:34-39). The dependency direction itself is a fitness test. CloudVendorIsolationTestsBase asserts that the core references no vendor SDK, beside a compile-time EnforceCloudVendorBoundary guard (ADR-134, Decision; restated in MMCA.Common/AGENTS.md, "Cloud vendor adapters"). [Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation] asks whether a decision is enforced rather than only written down, and this one is enforced twice.

    Where it is used. MMCA.ADC is the Azure adopter, and every call it makes comes from MMCA.Common.Azure and the MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup package it forwards. Every service host, the Gateway and the Blazor web host call AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration() and AddAzureMonitorExporter(), the service hosts mostly through the composed service host's ConfigureConfigurationSources and AfterServiceDefaults hooks (for example MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:70,75, MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:75,82). The four broker services register AddAzureServiceBusTransport() through RegisterBrokerTransports and call AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:228,325, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:85,140, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:128,260, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:91,97). Conference and Identity call AddAzureBlobFileStorage (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:301, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:225). The Notification module and the Identity host call AddAzureNotificationHubsPush (MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Notification/MMCA.ADC.Notification.API/DependencyInjection.cs:40, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:231). Identity and the Blazor web host call AddAzureDataProtection() (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:106, MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:47). No host under MMCA.ADC/Source calls an AWS or GCP adapter, or AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication. Those are exercised by Common's own LocalStack, emulator and Testcontainers tiers (aws-integration, gcp-integration, azure-integration in MMCA.Common/AGENTS.md), not by an MMCA.ADC deployment. The registration calls are C# extension(T) members, read as described in the primer.

    AwsClientEndpoint

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsClientEndpoint.cs:11 · Level 0 · class (internal static)

    • What it is: a one-method helper that points any AWS SDK ClientConfig at either a custom endpoint or an AWS region, from the ServiceUrl / Region pair every AWS settings section in this package carries.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; AWS SDK ClientConfig and RegionEndpoint.
    • Concept: one shared rule for endpoint resolution, so SNS, DynamoDB, S3 and Secrets Manager clients interpret the same two keys identically. [Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality] assesses whether duplicated logic is factored once; this helper is that single copy.
    • Walkthrough: Apply(config, serviceUrl, region) (AwsClientEndpoint.cs:23). An absolute serviceUrl wins: it sets config.ServiceURL (AwsClientEndpoint.cs:29) and, when a region is also given, uses it as the signing region via AuthenticationRegion (AwsClientEndpoint.cs:32). Otherwise a non-blank region sets RegionEndpoint (AwsClientEndpoint.cs:37). Neither set leaves the client on the SDK region fallback chain.
    • Why it's built this way: the doc comment records that the SDK treats the two as mutually exclusive (setting a region endpoint clears the service URL, AwsClientEndpoint.cs:16-17), hence the explicit branch rather than setting both. The endpoint override is what lets LocalStack, MinIO and Cloudflare R2 stand in for AWS (AwsClientEndpoint.cs:13-15).
    • Where it's used: SnsPushClientFactory (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Notifications/SnsPushClientFactory.cs:23,39), S3StorageClientFactory, and AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:118). The SQS broker transport uses it too, one step removed: AmazonSqsTransport resolves the region and an absolute ServiceUrl at registration (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:79,82) and hands both to AmazonSqsConnection (AmazonSqsTransport.cs:87), which applies them to its SQS and SNS client configs through this helper (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsConnection.cs:50,63).

    AwsStaticCredentials

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsStaticCredentials.cs:10 · Level 0 · class (internal static)

    • What it is: the shared both-or-neither rule for an AccessKeyId / SecretAccessKey pair.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; AWS SDK BasicAWSCredentials.
    • Concept: a half-configured credential is a configuration error, not a fallback. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether identity is explicit and least-surprise; the doc comment states the reason directly: silently falling back to the default chain "would run the host under an identity nobody configured" (AwsStaticCredentials.cs:15-16).
    • Walkthrough: Resolve(accessKeyId, secretAccessKey, sectionPath) (AwsStaticCredentials.cs:23) returns a BasicAWSCredentials when both halves are set (AwsStaticCredentials.cs:28-30), throws InvalidOperationException naming both keys under sectionPath when exactly one is set (AwsStaticCredentials.cs:33-36), and returns null when both are blank (AwsStaticCredentials.cs:39), which callers read as "use the AWS default credential chain".
    • Why it's built this way: the check runs at registration time in every caller, matching ADR-070: a misconfiguration surfaces at startup rather than on the first upload, send or query.
    • Where it's used: AwsServiceCollectionExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:65,118,188), AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:97), AmazonSqsTransport (AmazonSqsTransport.cs:80) and S3StorageClientFactory.

    S3ObjectLocation

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3ObjectLocation.cs:11 · Level 0 · record (internal sealed, positional)

    • What it is: the bucket and key of the one S3 object that holds the DataProtection key ring, parsed from the s3://bucket/key form of DataProtection:S3ObjectUri (S3ObjectLocation.cs:3-11).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; BCL only.
    • Concept: no new concept. It is the parse-once configuration value behind the gate-plus-receipt pattern introduced in AwsDataProtectionExtensions: the string is validated at registration, and every later step receives a typed value it can trust. The Cloud Storage counterpart is GcsObjectLocation.
    • Walkthrough: Parse(value, configurationKey) (S3ObjectLocation.cs:23) throws ArgumentNullException on null (S3ObjectLocation.cs:25), accepts the s3:// scheme case-insensitively (S3ObjectLocation.cs:27), splits the rest on its first /, and returns a location only when the bucket is non-empty, the key is non-empty and the key does not end in / (S3ObjectLocation.cs:29-33). Anything else throws InvalidOperationException naming the configuration key and the rejected value (S3ObjectLocation.cs:37-38). ToString() rebuilds the s3://bucket/key form (S3ObjectLocation.cs:42), which S3XmlRepository puts in its write-failure message.
    • Why it's built this way: the value is parsed by hand rather than through Uri, because Uri lower-cases and re-escapes an unknown scheme's authority and path in ways a bucket name or object key must not be (S3ObjectLocation.cs:5-7). A key ending in / is rejected because it names a folder, not an object (S3ObjectLocation.cs:16-17).
    • Where it's used: AwsDataProtectionExtensions parses it before registering anything (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:84), AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions takes it as a parameter (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:32), and S3XmlRepository holds it as Location (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3XmlRepository.cs:64). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/DataProtection/S3ObjectLocationTests.cs.

    AwsDataProtectionExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:21 · Level 1 · class (static, extension<TBuilder>)

    • What it is: AddAwsDataProtection(), which persists the ASP.NET Core DataProtection key ring to one Amazon S3 object so every replica of a scaled-out host shares one key ring (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:30-31).
    • Depends on: HostIntegrationMarker and HostIntegrationNames; the internal S3ObjectLocation (parses the s3://bucket/key form), AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions (PersistKeysToAmazonS3) and S3XmlRepository; externals IHostApplicationBuilder, AddDataProtection, and IAmazonS3 from AWSSDK.S3, with no DataProtection package of AWS's own.
    • Concept: a configuration-gated host integration with a startup receipt. The method is a no-op unless its gate key is set; when it does run, it registers a marker the core startup tripwire looks for, so a host that has the key but forgot the call fails instead of running every replica on its own key ring (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:86-88). It is declared as a C# extension<TBuilder> block (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:26-27; see C# extension(T) types). [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses whether scale-out does not break user sessions; a shared key ring is what keeps antiforgery tokens and auth cookies valid across replicas.
    • Walkthrough: reads DataProtection:S3ObjectUri (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:23, :72) and returns unchanged when blank (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:78-81), deliberately registering no receipt so an object configured by a later source still trips the check (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:74-77). Otherwise it parses the URI through S3ObjectLocation first, failing with the key named before anything is registered (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:83-84), registers HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsDataProtection) (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:88), resolves the application name from DataProtection:ApplicationName or the host's ApplicationName (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:90-91), and calls PersistKeysToAmazonS3(location, DataProtection:KmsKeyId) (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:93-95). That step sets the key manager's XmlRepository to an S3XmlRepository built when DataProtection first reads its options, over the IAmazonS3 the host registered or, failing that, a default client from the SDK default region and credential chain (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34-40).
    • Why it's built this way: the whole ring is one XML object, the shape the Azure Blob and Cloud Storage repositories use, written read-modify-write under an S3 conditional write: If-Match on the ETag it read, or If-None-Match: * to create it, so two replicas adding a key at once cannot overwrite each other; the loser re-reads and retries up to 10 times with a linear backoff (S3XmlRepository.cs:16-23, :39, :101, :105; detail in S3XmlRepository). DataProtection:KmsKeyId selects SSE-KMS for every write; without it the bucket's default encryption applies (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:40-42). The gate keeps developer machines and test hosts on the in-memory default with no AWS dependency at startup (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:35-39). The doc comment states that setting this object together with DataProtection:BlobStorageUri or DataProtection:GcsObjectUri fails the startup tripwire (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:61-63), one key ring per host, and the tripwire also fails a host that still sets the retired DataProtection:SsmParameterPrefix, naming DataProtection:S3ObjectUri as the replacement (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:123-126; see HostIntegrationTripwire). Recorded in ADR-069 and ADR-134; the Azure sibling is AzureDataProtectionExtensions and the Google Cloud sibling is GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.
    • Where it's used: no call site outside the defining file; no MMCA.ADC/Source host calls it. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/AwsDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the required IAM permissions (s3:GetObject and s3:PutObject on the object, s3:ListBucket on the bucket, plus kms:GenerateDataKey and kms:Decrypt when a KMS key is set) are stated in the doc comment (AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:54-58) and are not checked by code; without s3:ListBucket a not-yet-created object reads as 403 and the first start fails.

    S3XmlRepository

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3XmlRepository.cs:36 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed, IXmlRepository)

    • What it is: the DataProtection IXmlRepository that keeps the whole key ring as ONE S3 object: a <repository> document whose children are the key elements (S3XmlRepository.cs:11-14, :41).
    • Depends on: S3ObjectLocation; externals IXmlRepository (Microsoft.AspNetCore.DataProtection.Repositories), IAmazonS3, GetObjectRequest, PutObjectRequest and AmazonS3Exception from AWSSDK.S3, and System.Xml.Linq.
    • Concept introduced, optimistic concurrency through a conditional write. Several replicas may each decide to add a key at the same moment. Instead of a lock, every write states the version it is based on: a read returns the content and its ETag in one response, and the write carries If-Match with that ETag (or If-None-Match: * when the object did not exist yet, so the write can only create it). If another replica wrote in between, S3 refuses with 412 (or 409 when it sees the two conditional writes overlap), and the loser re-reads the ring, which now holds the winner's key, and tries again (S3XmlRepository.cs:16-23). Neither key is lost. This is the S3 counterpart of the Blob ETag and the Cloud Storage generation precondition; the Cloud Storage sibling is GcsXmlRepository. The rubric tag for the shared key ring sits on AwsDataProtectionExtensions.
    • Walkthrough:
      • Constants: MaxAttempts = 10 (S3XmlRepository.cs:39), the root element name repository (S3XmlRepository.cs:41), and UTF-8 writer settings without a byte order mark, so the object is plain XML text (S3XmlRepository.cs:43-44).
      • The constructor (S3XmlRepository.cs:55-61) null-checks the client and location and normalizes a blank KMS key id to null (S3XmlRepository.cs:59).
      • GetAllElements() (S3XmlRepository.cs:67-68) reads the document and returns the root's child elements.
      • StoreElement(element, friendlyName) (S3XmlRepository.cs:71) loops up to MaxAttempts (S3XmlRepository.cs:75): read the document and ETag (S3XmlRepository.cs:77), append a copy of the element so the caller's element is never re-parented into a document a retry discards (S3XmlRepository.cs:79-80), serialize it (S3XmlRepository.cs:82-88), and build a PutObjectRequest with ContentType = application/xml (S3XmlRepository.cs:90-97). The condition is IfNoneMatch = "*" when there was no ETag and IfMatch = eTag otherwise (S3XmlRepository.cs:99-106); a configured KMS key adds SSE-KMS (S3XmlRepository.cs:108-112). A 412 or 409 is caught (S3XmlRepository.cs:119) and the thread sleeps retryDelay * attempt before the next attempt, skipping the sleep after the last one (S3XmlRepository.cs:123-126). When every attempt loses, it throws InvalidOperationException naming the location and the attempt count (S3XmlRepository.cs:130-131).
      • ReadAsync() (S3XmlRepository.cs:143) returns the loaded document with the response ETag (S3XmlRepository.cs:147-151); a 404 yields an empty <repository> with a null ETag, so the first write is create-only (S3XmlRepository.cs:153-156).
      • Wait<T> (S3XmlRepository.cs:140) runs the SDK call on the thread pool and blocks on it, with VSTHRD002 suppressed (S3XmlRepository.cs:139-141), so a caller holding a synchronization context (a Blazor circuit) cannot deadlock it (S3XmlRepository.cs:134-137).
    • Why it's built this way: one object per ring rather than one per key matches the Blob and Cloud Storage repositories (S3XmlRepository.cs:11-13). Reading content and ETag from the same response guarantees the ETag a write is conditioned on is the one whose content it extended (S3XmlRepository.cs:22-23). Blocking sync-over-async is accepted because IXmlRepository is synchronous, the SDK is async only, and DataProtection reads the ring rarely (at startup and on its refresh period) and writes it only when it creates a key (S3XmlRepository.cs:30-33). See ADR-069.
    • Where it's used: constructed only by AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:36-40). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/DataProtection/S3XmlRepositoryTests.cs and MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/AwsDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the backoff is Thread.Sleep, so a contended write blocks its thread. With the 50 ms unit the builder passes (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:18), nine sleeps of 1 to 9 units add up to 2.25 s before the final failure; that figure is arithmetic on the cited values, not a measured number.

    AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:15 · Level 2 · class (internal static, classic this extension)

    • What it is: the internal PersistKeysToAmazonS3(location, kmsKeyId) step behind AddAwsDataProtection, shaped like the framework's own PersistKeysTo... calls (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:9-13, :32).
    • Depends on: S3ObjectLocation, S3XmlRepository; externals IDataProtectionBuilder, KeyManagementOptions, IConfigureOptions<T> / ConfigureOptions<T>, IAmazonS3 and AmazonS3Client.
    • Concept: no new concept. It defers client resolution through the options system: instead of building the repository at registration, it registers an IConfigureOptions<KeyManagementOptions> that runs when DataProtection first builds its options (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:11-13). The Google Cloud sibling is GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.
    • Walkthrough: RetryDelay is 50 ms, the backoff unit handed to the repository (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:18). PersistKeysToAmazonS3 (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:32) adds a singleton options configurator (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34-35) that sets XmlRepository to a new S3XmlRepository over the container's IAmazonS3, or a new AmazonS3Client() from the SDK defaults when there is none, plus the location, KMS key id and delay (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:36-40), then returns the builder (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:42).
    • Why it's built this way: the fallback client is deliberately NOT added to the container: a default client registered here would win the TryAdd in a later AddS3FileStorage and silently ignore its FileStorage endpoint settings (AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:24-26). Resolving at options time also means the host's own client (an emulator endpoint, or the one AddS3FileStorage registers, AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:21-23) is found regardless of registration order. See ADR-069.
    • Where it's used: only AwsDataProtectionExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:95).

    AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:23 · Level 2 · class (static, extension<TBuilder>)

    • What it is: AddAwsSecretsManagerConfiguration(), which adds an AWS Secrets Manager configuration source so every secret under one name prefix reads through IConfiguration like any other setting.
    • Depends on: AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationSource, AwsStaticCredentials, AwsClientEndpoint, HostIntegrationMarker, HostIntegrationNames; AWS SDK AmazonSecretsManagerClient.
    • Concept: runtime secrets as a configuration source, with the same gate-plus-receipt shape as AwsDataProtectionExtensions. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether secrets stay out of files and images; here production secrets are fetched from the vault at startup and optionally re-read on an interval.
    • Walkthrough: reads SecretsManager:NamePrefix (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:85) and returns unchanged when blank, registering no receipt (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:87-94). It then resolves the static key pair, failing before any source is added (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:96-98). ReloadIntervalMinutes, when present, must parse as a positive invariant-culture integer or the method throws naming the value (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:103-115). It builds an AmazonSecretsManagerConfig through AwsClientEndpoint.Apply (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:117-121), registers HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsSecretsManagerConfiguration) (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:125), and adds the source with a client factory, the prefix and the interval (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:127-130).
    • Why it's built this way: the naming convention matches the Key Vault sibling (AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions): the prefix is stripped and -- maps to the configuration separator, so adc/prod/ConnectionStrings--Default arrives as ConnectionStrings:Default (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:53-57), letting a secret set move between clouds by renaming its prefix. A malformed reload interval fails loudly rather than degrading to "never reload" (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:100-102). It is deliberately not called from AddServiceDefaults(), and because ConfigurationManager loads each source as it is added the secrets are read synchronously at this call, so it must run before the settings binding that reads them (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:67-73). See ADR-061 and ADR-134.
    • Where it's used: no call site outside the defining file; no MMCA.ADC/Source host calls it.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the claim that a secret the identity cannot read fails startup (AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:63-64) is enforced in the configuration provider, not in this file.

    AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:22 · Level 2 · class (static, extension<TBuilder>)

    • What it is: AddAwsTelemetryExporter(), which exports traces, and logs when a log group and stream are configured, straight to the AWS X-Ray and CloudWatch Logs OTLP endpoints, signed with AWS Signature Version 4, beside the vendor-neutral OTLP exporter AddServiceDefaults() configures. It is the AWS twin of AddAzureMonitorExporter() and AddGoogleCloudTelemetryExporter(), with no collector sidecar (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:16-21, :40-43).
    • Depends on: AwsTelemetrySettings, AwsStaticCredentials, IAwsTelemetryCredentialSource, DefaultChainAwsTelemetryCredentialSource, StaticAwsTelemetryCredentialSource, AwsTelemetryCredentialWarmup, AwsSigV4ExportHandler, HostIntegrationMarker, HostIntegrationNames; externals OpenTelemetry OtlpTraceExporter, OtlpLogExporter, BatchActivityExportProcessor, BatchLogRecordExportProcessor, and AWS SDK RegionEndpoint.
    • Concept: the gate-plus-receipt host integration introduced in AwsDataProtectionExtensions, applied to telemetry export, plus an ordering guard. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] assesses whether telemetry reaches the operator's backend in a usable shape; here traces and logs land in X-Ray and CloudWatch Logs directly, stamped with cloud.provider = aws and cloud.region resource attributes (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:143-147). See ADR-041.
    • Walkthrough:
      • Constants: the SigV4 service names xray and logs (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:25, :28) and the x-aws-log-group / x-aws-log-stream header names (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:31, :34).
      • AddAwsTelemetryExporter() (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:89) first throws unless the non-keyed HostIntegrationNames.ServiceDefaultsTelemetry marker is already registered, whether or not the region is set (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:91-99). It reads AwsTelemetrySettings (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:101) and returns with no exporter, no credential lookup and no receipt when AwsTelemetry:Region is blank (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:103-108).
      • Past the gate it resolves the region (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:110-111), the both-or-neither static key pair (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:112-115), the both-or-neither log group and stream (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:116, :212-223) and the two endpoints (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:117-118), then registers HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsTelemetryExporter) (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:122).
      • It picks a static or default-chain credential source (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:124-126); on the default chain it registers AwsTelemetryCredentialWarmup as a singleton and a hosted service (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:130-136).
      • It adds the resource attributes and a batch trace processor over an OtlpTraceExporter (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:142-150), and, only when logs are configured, a batch log processor over an OtlpLogExporter that adds the log group and stream headers (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:152-162).
      • ResolveTracesEndpoint and ResolveLogsEndpoint (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:176-177, :187-188) take a configured override as the full signal URL, which must be an absolute http or https URL or the call throws naming the key, and otherwise default to https://xray.{region}.amazonaws.com/v1/traces and https://logs.{region}.amazonaws.com/v1/logs (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:190-208).
      • CreateExporterOptions (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:234) sets OTLP over HTTP/protobuf with Headers = null (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:244-249) and an HttpClientFactory that wraps an AwsSigV4ExportHandler over an HttpClientHandler, keeping the exporter timeout and the container's TimeProvider or TimeProvider.System (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:242-258).
    • Why it's built this way: the exporters are built by hand rather than through AddOtlpExporter, because the OpenTelemetry SDK refuses a signal-specific AddOtlpExporter where UseOtlpExporter (the vendor-neutral exporter service defaults enable) also runs, and this one has to coexist with it (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:138-141). Clearing the headers keeps an OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS meant for the vendor-neutral exporter from being sent to AWS (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:229-232). The ordering guard exists because the outbox-poll and probe filter processors service defaults register must run before this exporter's batch processor, or the spans they un-record are exported anyway (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:77-80). Metrics are not exported: one request is capped at 1,000 data points and the OpenTelemetry .NET SDK offers no supported way to split one metric's points, so metrics go through an ADOT collector via OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:66-69). The default endpoints are for the commercial aws partition; China and GovCloud hosts set the overrides (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:55-56). The default credential chain is resolved once while the host starts, never at registration, and a failure there is logged as one Warning without failing the host (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:58-61). AwsTelemetry:Region set without this call fails the startup tripwire (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:56, :145-146). Recorded in ADR-041 and ADR-134; siblings AzureMonitorExporterExtensions and GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.
    • Where it's used: AwsSigV4ExportHandler reads the log group and stream header constants (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Telemetry/AwsSigV4ExportHandler.cs:38-39); no MMCA.ADC/Source host calls the method. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Telemetry/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensionsTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the doc comment states that the signature, headers, gating and wiring are covered by tests against the SDK signer and a loopback receiver, and that the call has not been exercised against live X-Ray or CloudWatch Logs endpoints (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:72-74).

    AwsServiceCollectionExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:30 · Level 7 · class (static, extension(IServiceCollection))

    • What it is: the public DI surface of the AWS adapter package: AddS3FileStorage, AddAmazonSqsTransport, AddAwsRdsIamAuthentication and AddAmazonSnsPush, each replacing a provider-neutral core default with an AWS implementation.
    • Depends on: S3FileStorageSettings, S3StorageClientFactory, S3FileStorageService, IFileStorageService, AmazonSqsTransport, RdsIamAuthenticationSettings, RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator, SnsNativePushSettings, SnsPushClientFactory, SnsInstallationRegistry, SnsNativePushSender, SnsPushDeviceRegistrar, INativePushSender, IPushDeviceRegistrar, AwsStaticCredentials, HostIntegrationMarker, HostIntegrationNames.
    • Concept introduced, the cloud-neutral core with vendor adapters. Core Common defines the abstractions and a safe default (an unconfigured file store, a null push sender, the broker transport registry, the Npgsql configurator hook); this package swaps in AWS implementations only when called and only when configured. Every method is configuration-gated, so a host can call it unconditionally and a deployment turns the capability on by configuration alone. Declared as a C# extension(IServiceCollection) block (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:32; see C# extension(T) types, ADR-106). [Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness] assesses vendor independence of the core; ADR-134 records the split, and ArchitectureRules.CloudVendors enforces it (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture/Rules/Layering/ArchitectureRules.CloudVendors.cs:98-99). [Rubric §1, SOLID] (open/closed): new providers arrive as packages, not core edits. Sibling surfaces: AzureServiceCollectionExtensions, GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.
    • Walkthrough:
      • AddS3FileStorage(configuration) (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:51) binds FileStorage options (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:55-56), is a no-op without a Bucket (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:58-62), checks the key pair at registration (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:65), then TryAddSingletons the S3 client and adds IFileStorageService as S3FileStorageService (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:67-68). Checksum settings default to WHEN_REQUIRED because SDK v4 sends integrity checksums by default and several S3-compatible services reject them (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:42-43). See ADR-045.
      • AddAmazonSqsTransport() (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:86-87) registers a new AmazonSqsTransport through AddBrokerTransport; it must be called before AddBrokerMessaging, which picks the transport (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:75-76). Its doc comment records that SQS caps a message delay at 15 minutes, so every second-level redelivery interval longer than that is shortened to it, and that with MessageBus:ProvisionTopology on (the default) the transport creates the SNS topics, the host's queue, its {queue}-error queue and the subscriptions itself (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:79-82). See ADR-066.
      • AddAwsRdsIamAuthentication(configuration) (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:108) is a no-op unless Rds:IamAuthentication:Enabled is true (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:112-116), resolves credentials (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:118-121), fails on any configured cleartext PostgreSQL connection string (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:123-125), resolves the optional region (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:127), registers the HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsRdsIamAuthentication) receipt only past the gate and only once, so the gate set without this call fails the startup tripwire instead of logging in with whatever password the connection string carries (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:129-138), and calls AddNpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator with a RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:140).
      • AddAmazonSnsPush(configuration) (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:174) is a no-op unless NativePush:Enabled parses true and the section IsComplete (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:178-185); it then resolves credentials (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:188), binds options (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:190), TryAddSingletons the SNS and DynamoDB clients and the registry (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:191-193), and adds the sender and registrar as transients (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:194-195). See ADR-044.
    • Why it's built this way: TryAddSingleton for SDK clients means a client the host registered first is used as is, the same posture as the Azure adapter (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:45-46,167-168). Every key-pair check runs at registration, not on first use (ADR-070). The configuration-pipeline integrations (secrets, data protection) live on the host builder instead, in AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions and AwsDataProtectionExtensions, because they act on builder.Configuration.
    • Where it's used: no call site outside the defining file; no MMCA.ADC/Source host calls any of the four methods (ADC runs on Azure). Tests call them, for example MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Notifications/AddAmazonSnsPushTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the IAM permissions listed for SNS push (AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:161-168) and the external provisioning of the table and platform applications are documentation only; no code verifies them.

    AmazonSqsTransportSettings

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:8 · Level 0 · class

    • What it is: the bound options class for the Amazon SQS/SNS broker transport, read from the MessageBus:AmazonSqs section (AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:11).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; System.Uri for the endpoint override.
    • Concept: a vendor-specific sub-section of the provider-neutral MessageBusSettings. The neutral settings carry the redelivery policy; this class carries only what SQS addressing needs. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether broker choice is a configuration concern rather than a code change; here the SQS addressing lives in its own section so the shared MessageBus keys stay transport-agnostic.
    • Walkthrough: four init properties. Region (AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:18) is a region system name; blank defers to the AWS SDK fallback chain, and no region at all fails registration (enforced by AmazonSqsTransport). ServiceUrl (AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:24) is one endpoint override for both SQS and SNS, honored only when absolute. AccessKeyId / SecretAccessKey (AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:31,34) are an optional static key pair, both-or-neither (enforced by AwsStaticCredentials).
    • Why it's built this way: every field is optional because the AWS default chains (region and credentials) are the production path for an IAM-role host; the static pair and endpoint override exist for LocalStack and non-AWS environments. Transport selection by configuration is recorded in ADR-066, the fail-at-registration posture in ADR-070.
    • Where it's used: read once inside AmazonSqsTransport.Configure (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:78-79) and named in its error messages (AmazonSqsTransport.cs:81,152).

    IAmazonSqsSettlement

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:8 · Level 0 · interface (internal)

    • What it is: the four SQS calls that settle one received message, abstracted so the settle logic in AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler never touches the SDK client directly.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; BCL Task, CancellationToken, IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string>.
    • Concept: a settlement port, the SQS counterpart of IAzureServiceBusSettlement. [Rubric §14, Testability] assesses whether behavior can be exercised without real infrastructure; with the broker calls behind this interface, every disposition branch of the handler is tested against a fake (MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandlerTests.cs).
    • Walkthrough: DeleteAsync removes the received message from the host's queue (IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:13). SendToQueueAsync sends a copy back to the same queue with a delay of 0 to 900 seconds (IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:21). SendToErrorQueueAsync sends a copy to the {queue}-error queue (IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:28). ChangeVisibilityAsync resets the message's visibility timeout from now: a heartbeat during processing, or zero to hand it straight back to the queue (IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:37). Headers are passed as a dictionary and packed into one attribute by the implementation (IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:17,25).
    • Why it's built this way: SQS has no native dead-letter or defer verb per message, so every disposition is composed from these primitives (send a copy, then delete). Keeping that composition in the handler and only the raw calls here makes the order of operations (copy first, delete second) testable in isolation.
    • Where it's used: implemented by QueueSettlement (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:273), consumed by AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.HandleAsync (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:62).

    AmazonSqsConnection

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsConnection.cs:13 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the owner of the one AmazonSQSClient and one AmazonSimpleNotificationServiceClient the transport uses, registered as a singleton so both clients are disposed with the container.
    • Depends on: AwsClientEndpoint; AWS SDK AmazonSQSClient, AmazonSimpleNotificationServiceClient, RegionEndpoint, AWSCredentials.
    • Concept: a connection holder shared by the publisher, the provisioner and the receiver; see AzureServiceBusConnection for the Azure sibling.
    • Walkthrough: the constructor builds an SQS and an SNS config, then creates each client with static credentials when supplied, else with the AWS default credential chain (AmazonSqsConnection.cs:22-33). Sqs and Sns expose the clients as their interfaces (AmazonSqsConnection.cs:36,39). CreateSqsConfig and CreateSnsConfig each start from an empty SDK config and hand it to AwsClientEndpoint.Apply with the optional endpoint override and the region's system name (AmazonSqsConnection.cs:45-52,58-65). Dispose disposes both clients (AmazonSqsConnection.cs:68-72).
    • Why it's built this way: one endpoint override serves both services, which is what LocalStack needs, and the config factories are internal static so tests can assert the endpoint and signing region without opening a connection.
    • Where it's used: registered by AmazonSqsTransport.Register (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:87) and resolved there for the provisioner, publisher and receiver (AmazonSqsTransport.cs:91,97,101); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransportTests.cs.

    AmazonSqsEntityNames

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:12 · Level 3 · class (internal static)

    • What it is: the naming rules that turn the framework's topic and queue names into legal SNS topic and SQS queue names.
    • Depends on: BrokerNaming.
    • Concept: per-vendor name fitting over the neutral BrokerNaming.Fit; the Azure sibling is AzureServiceBusEntityNames.
    • Walkthrough: three constants: MaxTopicNameLength = 256 (AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:15), MaxQueueNameLength = 80 (AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:18) and ErrorQueueSuffix = "-error" (AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:21). Topic fits the framework topic ({namespace}-{event}) to 256 characters (AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:26), Queue fits the topology's queue name to 80 (AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:31), and ErrorQueue appends -error before fitting to 80 (AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:36).
    • Why it's built this way: the limits are the SNS and SQS name caps, and fitting the error queue after the suffix is appended keeps it a legal name even for a queue name already at the cap.
    • Where it's used: AmazonSqsPublisher (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:34) and AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.ProvisionAsync (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:96-97,115); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyTests.cs.

    AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:27 · Level 6 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: hands one received SQS message to the framework's IBrokerReceivePipeline and carries out the BrokerDisposition it returns; it decides nothing itself (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:8-9).
    • Depends on: IBrokerReceivePipeline, BrokerDisposition, IAmazonSqsSettlement, AmazonSqsMessageMapping; BCL TimeProvider, ILogger; AWS SDK Message.
    • Concept: the vendor-side half of the framework's receive pipeline: policy (retries, redelivery, dead-lettering) is decided once in the neutral pipeline, and each transport only translates the verdict into broker calls; see AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler for the Azure translation. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses poison-message handling and delivery guarantees; here every disposition is "copy first, delete second", so a crash in between yields a duplicate the inbox absorbs rather than a lost message (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:12-15). [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses graceful behavior under failure and shutdown; a visibility heartbeat and a best-effort release on stop keep a message owned by exactly one live consumer.
    • Walkthrough: VisibilityTimeoutSeconds = 60 (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:33) and HeartbeatInterval at a third of it, 20 seconds, so two renewals can fail in a row before the message reappears (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:36). The constructor null-checks the pipeline, clock and logger (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:46-55). HandleAsync (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:62) maps the headers, starts RunHeartbeatAsync on a linked token, awaits ProcessAsync, swallows an OperationCanceledException only when the host is stopping, then stops and awaits the heartbeat (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:67-86). With no disposition (the host stopped) it calls ReleaseBestEffortAsync, which sets visibility to zero and only logs a failure (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:88-92,147-159). Completed deletes (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:97-99); Redeliver sends a copy to the queue with the redelivery count header and the delay in seconds, then deletes (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:101-108); DeadLetter sends a copy to the error queue with the reason and detail headers, then deletes (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:110-116); an unknown disposition throws, leaving the message to reappear (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:118-121). Settlement calls pass CancellationToken.None (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:98,106). RunHeartbeatAsync waits HeartbeatInterval on the injected TimeProvider and renews visibility to 60 seconds, logging and continuing on a failed renewal (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:125-145). Two [LoggerMessage] warnings, EventId 10 (release failed) and 11 (heartbeat failed) (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:161,164).
    • Why it's built this way: a short 60-second visibility timeout means a message whose consumer died is redelivered within a minute, while the heartbeat keeps a slow but live message hidden (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:29-32). Settling with no cancellation token is deliberate: the work is already done, and settling it during shutdown is what keeps it from running twice (AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:23-25). The second-level redelivery model is recorded in ADR-087.
    • Where it's used: constructed by AmazonSqsTransport.Register (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:103-106) and driven by AmazonSqsReceiver (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:195); VisibilityTimeoutSeconds also sets the receive request and the queue attribute (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:92, MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:189); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandlerTests.cs.

    AmazonSqsMessageMapping

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:19 · Level 6 · class (internal static)

    • What it is: the pure translation between the framework's broker messages and SNS/SQS requests and messages, including how headers travel.
    • Depends on: OutgoingBrokerMessage, IncomingBrokerMessage, BrokerHeaders, AmazonSqsTransport (for MaxDelaySeconds); BCL Utf8JsonWriter, JsonDocument; AWS SDK PublishRequest, SendMessageRequest, Message.
    • Concept: header packing. SQS and SNS cap a message at 10 attributes, so every header travels as one JSON object in a single mmca-headers string attribute (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:22; MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:24-25). The Azure sibling is AzureServiceBusMessageMapping. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses wire-format robustness; malformed header text yields no headers, and the pipeline then dead-letters the message as an unknown event instead of guessing (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:57-60).
    • Walkthrough: constants HeadersAttribute = "mmca-headers", DeadLetterReasonHeader, DeadLetterDetailHeader and MaxDeadLetterTextLength = 4096 (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:22,25,28,31). PackHeaders writes the pairs as a JSON object (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:38-55); UnpackHeaders keeps only string-valued properties, returns empty for non-object text and clears on a JsonException (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:64-94). ToPublishRequest builds the SNS publish with the UTF-8 body and the packed attribute (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:100-114). ToHeaders takes any other string attribute under its own name, then overlays the unpacked mmca-headers, so the framework's own headers win (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:123-152). ToIncoming builds the IncomingBrokerMessage from body, headers and MessageId (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:158-167). RedeliveryHeaders copies the headers and sets BrokerHeaders.RedeliveryCount (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:176-184). DeadLetterHeaders adds the reason and, when present, the detail (removing a stale detail otherwise), each cut to 4096 characters (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:195-213,249-250). ToSendRequest builds an SQS send with the body, DelaySeconds and the packed attribute (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:221-237). ToDelaySeconds rounds the delay up to whole seconds and clamps it to 0 to 900 (AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:246-247).
    • Why it's built this way: packing all headers into one attribute removes the 10-attribute ceiling entirely, and keeping the mapping static and side-effect free lets the wire format be pinned by unit tests.
    • Where it's used: AmazonSqsPublisher (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:36), AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:67,75,104-105,113) and QueueSettlement (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:279,282); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsMessageMappingTests.cs.

    AmazonSqsPublisher

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:12 · Level 6 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the SNS implementation of IBrokerPublisher: one publish per outgoing message to the event's topic.
    • Depends on: IBrokerPublisher, OutgoingBrokerMessage, AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner, AmazonSqsEntityNames, AmazonSqsMessageMapping; AWS SDK IAmazonSimpleNotificationService.
    • Concept: fan-out by topic. A publisher only knows topics; each subscribing host's queue receives a copy through its SNS subscription. See AzureServiceBusPublisher for the Azure sibling.
    • Walkthrough: the constructor null-checks the SNS client and the provisioner (AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:20-27). PublishAsync (AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:30) resolves the topic ARN through GetTopicArnAsync on the fitted topic name (AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:34), then publishes the mapped request (AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:36).
    • Why it's built this way: delegating topic resolution to the provisioner means a missing topic is created on the first publish when MessageBus:ProvisionTopology is on, and fails with a named error when it is off (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:69-78); the ARN is cached after the first lookup.
    • Where it's used: registered as the IBrokerPublisher singleton by AmazonSqsTransport.Register (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:96-98); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyTests.cs and AmazonSqsTransportTests.cs.

    AmazonSqsReceiver

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:20 · Level 6 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: the hosted BackgroundService that provisions the host's queues, long-polls its SQS queue and runs each message through AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler with bounded concurrency.
    • Depends on: BrokerTopology, MessageBusSettings, BrokerTransportPolicies, AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler, AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner, QueueSettlement; AWS SDK IAmazonSQS; BCL BackgroundService, SemaphoreSlim.
    • Concept: a hand-written pull consumer. SQS has no push processor, so the receive loop, backpressure and shutdown drain live here; compare the Azure processor-based AzureServiceBusReceiver. [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] assesses throughput controls and backpressure; a semaphore of ConcurrentMessageLimit slots caps in-flight work and each receive asks only for as many messages as there are free slots. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] assesses diagnosable failure; every failure path logs through a source-generated [LoggerMessage] (EventIds 20 to 23, AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:260-270).
    • Walkthrough: constants DefaultConcurrentMessageLimit = 16, MaxMessagesPerReceive = 10 and WaitTimeSeconds = 20 (the SQS long-poll maximum) (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:23,26,29); backoff runs from 2 seconds to 1 minute (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:31-32). ResolveConcurrency reads MessageBus:ConcurrentMessageLimit, else 16 (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:74-75); ResolveBatchSize reads MessageBus:PrefetchCount, else 10, clamped to 1 to 10 (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:80-81). BuildReceiveRequest asks for every message attribute with the 60-second visibility timeout (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:87-94). ExecuteAsync returns at once when the host has no subscriptions, provisions with retry, then runs the loop and, in finally, drains by acquiring every slot (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:97-128). ReceiveLoopAsync waits for one slot, takes any others free right now with a zero timeout, receives up to that many, returns unused slots, and starts one Task.Run per message (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:130-189); a receive failure releases the slots, logs and backs off (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:164-176). ProcessAsync calls the handler, logs a settle failure and always releases its slot (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:191-207). ProvisionWithRetryAsync retries AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.ProvisionAsync with doubling backoff until it succeeds or the host stops (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:214-243,245).
    • Why it's built this way: neither provisioning nor a receive failure may crash the host, so both log and back off (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:209-213), and the drain on stop guarantees every in-flight handler has released its message before the service returns (AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:121-126).
    • Where it's used: registered with AddHostedService by AmazonSqsTransport.Register (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:100-109); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyTests.cs and AmazonSqsTransportTests.cs.

    AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:24 · Level 6 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: creates (or, with provisioning off, only looks up) the SNS topics, the host's SQS queue and error queue, the queue policy and the raw-delivery subscriptions.
    • Depends on: BrokerTopology, AmazonSqsEntityNames, AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler (for the visibility timeout); AWS SDK IAmazonSQS, IAmazonSimpleNotificationService; BCL ConcurrentDictionary, Utf8JsonWriter.
    • Concept: idempotent infrastructure-as-startup, the SQS counterpart of AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses least privilege; the queue policy grants sqs:SendMessage to the SNS service principal only when aws:SourceArn equals one of the subscribed topics, and to nothing else (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:144-180). [Rubric §17, DevOps] assesses environment setup without manual steps; with MessageBus:ProvisionTopology on, a fresh account gets its whole topology on first start.
    • Walkthrough: ErrorQueueRetentionSeconds = 1_209_600 (14 days, the SQS maximum) (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:27); topic ARNs are cached in a ConcurrentDictionary (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:33). GetTopicArnAsync returns a cached ARN, else creates the topic when provisioning or finds it otherwise, throwing a message that names MessageBus:ProvisionTopology when it is missing (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:59-81). ProvisionAsync (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:92) with provisioning off only looks up the two queue URLs (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:99-104); otherwise it ensures the error queue and the queue, reads the queue ARN, resolves every distinct consumed topic, sets the queue policy, then subscribes the queue to each topic (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:106-131) and logs EventId 1 (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:133,270). QueueAttributes sets the 60-second visibility timeout and ErrorQueueAttributes the 14-day retention (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:187-190,194-197). EnsureQueueAsync falls back to GetQueueUrl on QueueNameExistsException, leaving a queue created elsewhere as it is (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:203-214). EnsureRawSubscriptionAsync subscribes with RawMessageDelivery = true; on InvalidParameterException it finds the existing subscription by paging ListSubscriptionsByTopic and switches it to raw delivery (AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:220-245,247-268).
