    • Concept: the trivial sibling of DefaultChainAwsTelemetryCredentialSource; see IAwsTelemetryCredentialSource for the port.
    • Walkthrough: GetAsync wraps the credential in ValueTask.FromResult (:10-11); Get returns it (:14). Neither ever returns null or throws.
    • Where it's used: constructed by AddAwsTelemetryExporter() when AwsStaticCredentials resolves AwsTelemetry:AccessKeyId and AwsTelemetry:SecretAccessKey (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:112-115, :126); with it, no AwsTelemetryCredentialWarmup is registered (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:130).

    AwsSigV4ExportHandler

    MMCA.Common.Aws · MMCA.Common.Aws.Telemetry · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Telemetry/AwsSigV4ExportHandler.cs:30 · Level 2 · class (internal sealed partial, DelegatingHandler)

    • What it is: the DelegatingHandler under each AWS OTLP exporter's HttpClient that signs every export request with AWS Signature Version 4 for X-Ray (xray) or CloudWatch Logs (logs), and refuses to send when it cannot sign (AwsSigV4ExportHandler.cs:30-327).
    • Depends on: IAwsTelemetryCredentialSource, CredentialFailure, AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions (for the log-destination header names, :36-40); externals AWSSigV4Signer, AWSSigningRequest, AWSSigV4Parameters, RegionEndpoint, TimeProvider, [LoggerMessage].
    • Concept: fail closed on authentication, warn once per failure kind. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether outbound calls are authenticated and never degrade to an unauthenticated form: when the credential is missing, its lookup throws, or signing throws, the handler returns a synthesized 401 Unauthorized without calling the inner handler (:179-180), and the log text states that no unsigned request leaves the process (:231-235). [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] assesses whether failures are visible without flooding: each failure kind logs one Warning (event 1) for the handler's lifetime and Debug (event 2) after that, through Interlocked.Exchange on a per-kind flag (:304-315).
    • Walkthrough:
      • Constants and fields (:33-58): content-type is the one signed content header (:33); the CloudWatch Logs group and stream headers are signed when present (:36-40); three warn-once flags (:54-58).
      • Constructor (:70-90): guards every argument (service must be non-blank) and copies addedHeaders, the headers set on every request BEFORE signing (the logs exporter's x-aws-log-group / x-aws-log-stream, :66-68).
      • Send (:93-133): the synchronous path the exporter uses on its own background thread resolves the credential with Get (:102), refuses on a throw (:104-108) or null (:110-114), buffers the body (:116), signs with AWSSigV4Signer.Sign (:122), refuses on a signing failure (:124-128), applies the signature (:130) and sends (:132).
      • SendAsync (:136-177): the same steps with GetAsync (:143), ReadAsByteArrayAsync (:157-160) and SignAsync (:164-166).
      • Body handling: ReadBody (:183-196) and ReplaceContent (:202-219) swap the content for a ByteArrayContent copy with the same content headers (minus Content-Length), so the bytes on the wire are exactly the bytes hashed (:198-201).
      • PrepareSigningRequest (:262-292): adds the configured headers, then signs only content-type and the present log-destination headers; the signer adds host and its own x-amz-* headers (:257-261).
      • Parameters (:294-301): region, service, SignPayload = true, and SignedAt from the injected TimeProvider.
      • ApplySignature (:221-229): copies every signer header except host onto the request, replacing existing values.
      • Describe (:244-255): the reason text per CredentialFailure, with the exception type and message appended (never key material, :243).
    • Why it's built this way: the signed header set is fixed rather than "everything present", so headers the OTLP exporter or an intermediate handler adds cannot invalidate the signature (:257-261). A refreshing credential is resolved by the signer itself, so a failed refresh surfaces as a signing failure (:120-121). Recorded in ADR-041 and ADR-134; the GCP counterpart is GoogleCloudTelemetryAuthHandler.
    • Where it's used: built inside the HttpClientFactory of each AWS exporter's OtlpExporterOptions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:234-255), with service xray or logs (AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:25, :28).

    AzureServiceCollectionExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:31 · Level 7 · class (static, extension(IServiceCollection))

    • What it is: the Azure adapter's DI entry points: AddAzureBlobFileStorage, AddAzureNotificationHubsPush, AddAzureServiceBusTransport, AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication and AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:44, :95, :154, :181, :230).
    • Depends on: AzureBlobFileStorageSettings, AzureBlobFileStorageService, AzureNotificationHubSettings, NotificationHubConnection, INotificationHubRestClient, NotificationHubRestClient, NotificationHubRetryPolicy, AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender, AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar, AzureServiceBusTransport, AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings, AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator, AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings, AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator, AzureTokenCredentials, HostIntegrationMarker, HostIntegrationNames, IFileStorageService, INativePushSender, IPushDeviceRegistrar, IBrokerTransport, INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator, IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator; externals BlobServiceClient, DefaultAzureCredential, IHttpClientFactory and ResilienceHandler.
    • Concept: config-gated, unconditionally-callable registrations through extension(IServiceCollection) members (C# extension(T) types, ADR-106). [Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness] assesses whether infrastructure is swappable per deployment: each call is a no-op when its section is incomplete or disabled, so hosts call it always and deployments switch it on by configuration alone. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether data stores are reached with identities rather than shared secrets: the two Entra members put PostgreSQL and Redis on token login.
    • Walkthrough:
      • AddAzureBlobFileStorage (:44-74): binds options (:48-49), returns when ContainerName is blank (:52-55) or when there is neither an absolute ServiceUri nor a connection string (:58-62; an empty-string ServiceUri binds as a relative Uri, :57). Registers a BlobContainerClient with TryAddSingleton, preferring ServiceUri + DefaultAzureCredential over the connection string (:64-70), then the transient service (:71).
      • AddAzureNotificationHubsPush (:95-137): binds options, requires Enabled, ConnectionString and HubName (:103-108), registers the parsed NotificationHubConnection via TryAddSingleton (:110) and the internal REST client as a typed HttpClient built with NotificationHubRetryPolicy.Default (:111-116). RemoveAllLoggers() drops the factory's per-request logging because hub URIs carry user tags and installation ids (:117-120), and a handler filter removes every ResilienceHandler (:121-132) since the client carries its own retry (three retries with exponential backoff or the hub's Retry-After) and a 60-second attempt timeout (:87-90). Then the internal sender and registrar (:133-134), replacing the core null pair (ADR-044).
      • AddAzureServiceBusTransport (:154-155): services.AddBrokerTransport(new AzureServiceBusTransport()); the doc comment says call it BEFORE AddBrokerMessaging, which selects the transport by MessageBus:Provider=AzureServiceBus (ADR-066). With MessageBus:AzureServiceBus:FullyQualifiedNamespace set the bus signs in with an Entra token (optionally pinned by ManagedIdentityClientId) and ignores any connection string; otherwise the connection string resolves from MessageBus:ConnectionString, ConnectionStrings:rabbitmq, then ConnectionStrings:messaging; with neither, AddBrokerMessaging fails naming both (:142-150).
      • AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication (:181-208): returns unless AzurePostgreSql:EntraAuthentication:Enabled is true (:185-191); fails registration for any configured PostgreSQL connection string that does not require TLS (:193-195); adds the AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication receipt once (:197-205); registers an AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator through AddNpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator (:207). The doc comment states the scope (https://ossrdbms-aad.database.windows.net/.default), the four-minute refresh and the no-password, TLS-required connection string contract (:158-175).
      • AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication (:230-254): returns unless AzureRedis:EntraAuthentication:Enabled is true (:234-240); adds the AzureRedisEntraAuthentication receipt once (:242-250); registers an AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator over AzureTokenCredentials.Create(settings.ManagedIdentityClientId) through AddRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator (:252-253). It reaches the Redis client, distributed cache, output-cache store, SignalR backplane and the health check's connection (:211-213); a connection string with a password or ssl=false fails when its connection is configured (:223-225).
    • Why it's built this way: ADR-045, ADR-070 and ADR-134 place vendor code in adapters over core ports. The TryAdd client registration lets a host supply its own client first. The two Entra members check for an existing marker before adding one, so calling them twice registers one receipt, and the core startup tripwire fails a host that enables the gate without the call (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:151-152, :166-167). The PostgreSQL TLS check runs at registration so an unencrypted connection string fails before the first query (:193-194); PostgreSQL as an engine is ADR-113.
    • Where it's used: blob storage at MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:225 and MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:301; push at MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Notification/MMCA.ADC.Notification.API/DependencyInjection.cs:40 and MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:231; Service Bus in every ADC service host (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:260, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:325, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:140, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:97); Redis Entra in the same four (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:128, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:228, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:85, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:91). No MMCA.ADC/Source host calls AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication.

    AzureServiceBusEndpoint

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusEndpoint.cs:14 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the resolved answer to "where does the Service Bus transport connect": either a fully qualified namespace plus a Microsoft Entra credential, or a data-plane connection string plus the management-plane one (which, for the local emulator, points at a different port). It is computed and validated once while AddBrokerMessaging runs, and creating the clients from it performs no I/O (AzureServiceBusEndpoint.cs:7-13).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party. Externals: Azure.Core.TokenCredential, Azure.Messaging.ServiceBus.ServiceBusClient and ServiceBusAdministrationClient. Built only by AzureServiceBusTransport.ResolveEndpoint.
    • Concept introduced, a connection description that cannot leak its secret. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses how secrets are kept out of logs and how a service authenticates: the type is deliberately a class and not a record, because a record's generated ToString would print a connection string's shared access key into any log line that formats it (:11-12), and the token path carries no key at all.
    • Walkthrough: the constructor (:25-37) takes five values and stores them unchanged into five get-only properties: FullyQualifiedNamespace (:40), Credential (:43), ConnectionString (:46), AdministrationConnectionString (:49) and IsEmulator (:52). Exactly one of the two shapes is populated: the token shape sets the namespace and credential and leaves both strings null; the connection-string shape does the reverse. CreateClient (:56-59) and CreateAdministrationClient (:63-66) branch on FullyQualifiedNamespace is not null, so the namespace path always wins when present; on the connection-string path the data plane uses ConnectionString and the management plane uses AdministrationConnectionString. The caller owns the returned client.
    • Why it's built this way: resolving the endpoint at registration time rather than on first use means a misconfigured host fails at startup with a message, not at its first publish (AzureServiceBusTransport AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:74-76). IsEmulator is the single flag the rest of the transport reads to decide whether entity quotas apply (ServiceBusEmulatorSupport).
    • Where it's used: wrapped by AzureServiceBusConnection (AzureServiceBusConnection.cs:15-22); IsEmulator is passed to AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:87). Exercised through MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusReceiverTests.cs.

    AzureServiceBusTransportSettings

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransportSettings.cs:7 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the Service Bus transport's own configuration keys, bound from MessageBus:AzureServiceBus (SectionName, AzureServiceBusTransportSettings.cs:10): the namespace a host signs in to with a Microsoft Entra token instead of a shared access key.
    • Depends on: nothing. The shared broker keys (connection string, emulator admin endpoint, provisioning, backpressure) live on MessageBusSettings; this class carries only what is Azure-specific.
    • Concept introduced: none new; it is the settings half of the managed-identity sign-in that AzureServiceBusTransport introduces. [Rubric §11, Security] applies: a namespace host name plus an identity client id is enough to connect, so the namespace can run with shared access keys switched off.
    • Walkthrough: FullyQualifiedNamespace (:17) accepts a host name such as contoso.servicebus.windows.net or an sb:// address; when set, the bus signs in with a token and any broker connection string is ignored (:12-16). ManagedIdentityClientId (:24) pins the user-assigned identity; blank selects the system-assigned identity (or the one AZURE_CLIENT_ID names), and it is read only alongside the namespace (:19-23). Both are init-only.
    • Where it's used: bound by AzureServiceBusTransport.ReadSettings (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:171-177), with SectionName also composing the FullyQualifiedNamespaceKey error-message key (:43). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransportCredentialTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: which hosts set a namespace versus a connection string in each deployment is configuration, Not determinable from source.

    IAzureServiceBusSettlement

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/IAzureServiceBusSettlement.cs:10 · Level 0 · interface (internal)

    • What it is: the four broker calls that settle one delivered Service Bus message, behind an interface so the disposition logic in AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler is unit-testable without a broker (IAzureServiceBusSettlement.cs:5-9). It is the Azure counterpart of IAmazonSqsSettlement.
    • Depends on: external Azure.Messaging.ServiceBus.ServiceBusMessage only.
    • Concept introduced: [Rubric §14, Testability] assesses whether behavior can be tested in isolation: settling is the one part of a receive that needs a live lock token, so extracting just those calls lets every disposition branch be tested against a fake.
    • Walkthrough: CompleteAsync (:15) removes the message; DeadLetterAsync(reason, description, ...) (:22) moves it to the queue's native dead-letter queue; AbandonAsync (:27) releases the lock so the broker delivers it again; ScheduleOnQueueAsync(message, enqueueTime, ...) (:34) schedules a new message on the host's own queue, which is how a second-level redelivery is expressed.
    • Where it's used: implemented by ProcessorSettlement inside AzureServiceBusReceiver; consumed by AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler.HandleAsync; faked in MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandlerTests.cs.

    ServiceBusEmulatorSupport

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/ServiceBusEmulatorSupport.cs:26 · Level 0 · class (internal, static)

    • What it is: the pieces the Azure Service Bus transport needs to run against the official local emulator instead of a real namespace, kept in one place so the production path reads only one flag, AzureServiceBusEndpoint.IsEmulator, set from the connection string (ServiceBusEmulatorSupport.cs:6-9). It answers two questions: is this connection string an emulator one, and if so, what is the management-plane connection string.

    • Depends on: nothing first-party. External: Azure.Messaging.ServiceBus.Administration (named in the docs for ServiceBusAdministrationClient), BCL Uri and CultureInfo (ServiceBusEmulatorSupport.cs:1-2). Its input comes from MessageBusSettings (ConnectionString plus EmulatorAdminEndpoint).

    • Concept introduced, detection that keys off the artifact rather than the environment. [Rubric §17, DevOps] assesses whether development and production run the same paths: the point of the emulator branch is that a local stack exercises the same transport production uses, instead of substituting RabbitMQ and discovering Service Bus specific behavior only after deployment. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses blast radius: the branch is entered only when the resolved connection string carries UseDevelopmentEmulator=true, a token a real namespace never emits, so no production deployment can reach any of this by accident. That is stated as the reason detection is not keyed off an environment name or a separate flag, either of which somebody can set in the wrong place (ServiceBusEmulatorSupport.cs:10-16). The emulator differs from the real service in two ways the type accounts for: it serves the data plane (AMQP) and the management plane (HTTP) on two ports, so the administration client needs its own connection string, and it enforces a one-hour ceiling on entity time-to-live and auto-delete, so every entity provisioned there must stay beneath it (:17-24).

    • Walkthrough:

      • The two constants. EmulatorMarker is the literal "UseDevelopmentEmulator=true" (ServiceBusEmulatorSupport.cs:31). EmulatorEntityQuota is one hour, the ceiling the emulator enforces on entity time-to-live and auto-delete-on-idle (:36). The type holds no mutable state.
      • IsEmulatorConnectionString (:44-46) is the whole detection: a null-guard and a Contains with StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase, so casing in the connection string cannot smuggle a stack onto the wrong branch.
      • BuildAdminConnectionString (:59-84) derives the management-plane string from the AMQP one by swapping in the admin endpoint's host and port. The validation is stricter than "is it an absolute URI" and says why in a comment (:61-63): localhost:5300 is an absolute URI, with localhost read as the scheme and no host at all, so the check also requires the scheme to be http or https (:64-67). A missing or malformed endpoint throws an InvalidOperationException whose message names the setting, the reason (the transport provisions topics, subscriptions and queues through a management-plane client, which the emulator serves on a different port) and the fix, including that an Aspire AppHost gets it for free (:69-70). The rebuild itself formats Endpoint=sb://{host}:{port} with the invariant culture (:73-75), then splits on ; and replaces only the Endpoint= segment, leaving everything else in place (:77-83). That is what keeps the shared-access key name and value byte-identical across both clients: composing a fresh second string is one silent typo away from an admin client that cannot provision anything (:48-53).
      • Where the quota is applied. This type only defines EmulatorEntityQuota; AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner sets it as DefaultMessageTimeToLive on each topic, queue and subscription it creates when the endpoint is the emulator (AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner.cs:92-100, :111-124, :135-144). Nothing process-global is changed, so a production entity keeps the service defaults.

    • Why it's built this way: ADR-066 records the local Service Bus emulator path as part of transport selection and dev/prod parity. Keeping detection and the admin-string derivation in one internal static type is what lets AzureServiceBusTransport.ResolveEndpoint read the emulator case as one explicit branch that produces an ordinary AzureServiceBusEndpoint, after which nothing else in the transport needs to know.

    • Where it's used: AzureServiceBusTransport.ResolveEndpoint calls IsEmulatorConnectionString and, on a match, BuildAdminConnectionString(connectionString, context.Settings.EmulatorAdminEndpoint) (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:145-153); AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner reads EmulatorEntityQuota. The admin endpoint is bound from MessageBus:EmulatorAdminEndpoint (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:54), which an Aspire AppHost sets from the emulator resource (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:352). The test tier's emulator fixture, ServiceBusEmulatorFixtureBase, pins the emulator image (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.Azure/Fixtures/ServiceBusEmulatorFixtureBase.cs:64). MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/ServiceBusEmulatorSupportTests.cs covers marker detection, the endpoint swap, the loud failure on a missing or non-HTTP admin endpoint, and the quota constant.

    • Caveats / not-in-source: whether a given AppHost run uses the emulator or RabbitMQ is a host and environment decision, so "which transport a developer is on right now" is Not determinable from source here; the source only settles that the emulator branch is reachable exactly when the resolved connection string carries the marker and no fully qualified namespace is configured.

    AzureServiceBusConnection

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusConnection.cs:11 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed, IAsyncDisposable)

    • What it is: the process-wide Service Bus clients: one AMQP connection shared by the publisher and the receiver, plus the management-plane client. It is a singleton the container disposes on shutdown, which closes every sender and processor created from it (AzureServiceBusConnection.cs:6-10).
    • Depends on: AzureServiceBusEndpoint; externals ServiceBusClient, ServiceBusAdministrationClient.
    • Concept introduced: [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] assesses connection reuse: a ServiceBusClient owns one AMQP connection, so sharing a single instance across publishing and receiving keeps a host at one connection regardless of how many topics it sends to.
    • Walkthrough: the constructor (:15-22) null-guards the endpoint, stores it as Endpoint (:25), and creates Client (:28) and Administration (:31) through the endpoint's factory methods. DisposeAsync (:34) disposes only the data-plane client; the administration client is HTTP-based and holds no connection to close.
    • Where it's used: registered as a singleton by AzureServiceBusTransport.Register (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:81); its Administration feeds AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner (:85-88), its Client feeds AzureServiceBusPublisher (:91-93), and the whole instance is handed to AzureServiceBusReceiver (:95-96).

    ProcessorSettlement

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusReceiver.cs:202 · Level 1 · class (private sealed, nested in AzureServiceBusReceiver)

    • What it is: the receiver's IAzureServiceBusSettlement: a primary-constructor class over the processor's ProcessMessageEventArgs and the host queue's ServiceBusSender (AzureServiceBusReceiver.cs:202).
    • Depends on: IAzureServiceBusSettlement; externals ProcessMessageEventArgs, ServiceBusSender.
    • Concept introduced: none new; it is the production adapter behind the testability split that IAzureServiceBusSettlement introduces.
    • Walkthrough: each member is one SDK call. CompleteAsync calls args.CompleteMessageAsync (:204-205), DeadLetterAsync calls args.DeadLetterMessageAsync with the reason and description (:207-208), AbandonAsync calls args.AbandonMessageAsync with no property changes (:210-211), and ScheduleOnQueueAsync calls queueSender.ScheduleMessageAsync (:213-214), so a redelivery lands on the same queue the processor reads.
    • Where it's used: created per message in AzureServiceBusReceiver.ExecuteAsync (AzureServiceBusReceiver.cs:115-116) and passed to AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler.HandleAsync. No usage outside its defining file.

    AzureServiceBusMessageMapping

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusMessageMapping.cs:12 · Level 2 · class (internal, static)

    • What it is: the translation between the framework's broker messages and Service Bus messages. Every framework header travels as a string application property under its own key, so the receiver hands the pipeline exactly the keys and values the publisher wrote (AzureServiceBusMessageMapping.cs:7-11).
    • Depends on: OutgoingBrokerMessage, IncomingBrokerMessage, BrokerHeaders; externals ServiceBusMessage, ServiceBusReceivedMessage, CultureInfo.
    • Concept introduced: [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether the wire contract is explicit and transport-independent: the framework owns the headers, and this mapping only moves them into the one place Service Bus carries arbitrary metadata, so the same message reads identically on every transport.
    • Walkthrough:
      • ToServiceBusMessage (:17-34) builds the message from the body, sets MessageId to the framework message id formatted "D" with the invariant culture, ContentType to BrokerHeaders.JsonContentType and Subject to the event name (:21-26), then copies every header into ApplicationProperties (:28-31).
      • ToHeaders (:43-58) reads application properties back as an ordinal dictionary. A null value is skipped before conversion because Convert.ToString would turn it into an empty string; a non-string value written by another publisher is formatted with the invariant culture (:51-53).
      • ToIncoming (:63-71) produces the IncomingBrokerMessage the pipeline processes from the body, the headers and the message id (empty when absent) (:67-70).
      • CreateRedelivery (:81-100) copies body, message id, content type and subject (:85-90) and every application property (:92-95), then overwrites BrokerHeaders.RedeliveryCount with the new count (:97). Keeping the original message id is deliberate: the consumer inbox de-duplicates on it.
    • Where it's used: AzureServiceBusPublisher (AzureServiceBusPublisher.cs:44) and AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler (AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler.cs:61, :76). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusMessageMappingTests.cs.

    AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler.cs:24 · Level 3 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: the step between a delivered Service Bus message and the framework's IBrokerReceivePipeline: it hands the message in and carries out the BrokerDisposition that comes back. It decides nothing itself (AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler.cs:7-23). The Amazon counterpart is AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.
    • Depends on: IBrokerReceivePipeline, BrokerDisposition, IAzureServiceBusSettlement, AzureServiceBusMessageMapping; externals TimeProvider, ILogger.
    • Concept introduced, settle by disposition, never by judgment. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether retry and poison handling are consistent across transports: the policy (in-process retry, second-level redelivery, dead-lettering) lives once in the shared pipeline, and each transport only maps its three outcomes onto native calls. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] applies to the order of those calls: a redelivery schedules the copy before completing the original, so a crash in between yields a duplicate the inbox absorbs, never a lost message (:13-15).
    • Walkthrough:
      • State. MaxDeadLetterTextLength = 4096 (:27), the longest reason or description the SDK accepts; the pipeline, a TimeProvider and a logger (:29-31), all null-guarded in the constructor (:37).
      • HandleAsync (:53-95) maps the message and calls _pipeline.ProcessAsync (:61). An OperationCanceledException raised because the host is stopping is caught and turned into a best-effort abandon (:63-67). Otherwise it switches on the disposition: Completed completes (:71-73); Redeliver schedules CreateRedelivery(message, redeliver.RedeliveryCount) at _timeProvider.GetUtcNow() + redeliver.Delay and then completes the original (:75-81); DeadLetter dead-letters with the truncated reason and detail (:83-88); any other disposition throws InvalidOperationException, treated as a framework bug so the processor abandons and reports rather than guesses (:90-93). Every settle call passes CancellationToken.None: the work is done, and settling it during shutdown is what keeps it from running twice (:21-22).
      • Truncate (:100-101) cuts text longer than 4096 characters.
      • AbandonBestEffortAsync (:103-115) swallows and logs any abandon failure under a scoped CA1031 suppression (:109): an unabandoned message is redelivered once its lock expires anyway. LogAbandonFailed is event id 10 at Warning (:117-118).
    • Why it's built this way: ADR-087 records second-level redelivery and poison handling, applied unconditionally on Service Bus because it schedules natively.
    • Where it's used: constructed per host by AzureServiceBusTransport.Register (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:98-101) and invoked by AzureServiceBusReceiver for every message (AzureServiceBusReceiver.cs:115-116). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandlerTests.cs.

    AzureServiceBusEntityNames

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusEntityNames.cs:11 · Level 3 · class (internal, static)

    • What it is: the Service Bus entity names the transport derives from the framework's broker names. Service Bus caps a queue or topic path at 260 characters and a subscription name at 50, so each name goes through BrokerNaming.Fit: unchanged when it fits, otherwise shortened deterministically so the publisher and every consumer compute the same entity (AzureServiceBusEntityNames.cs:5-10). Sibling of AmazonSqsEntityNames.
    • Depends on: BrokerNaming.
    • Concept introduced: none new; deterministic fitting is taught at BrokerNaming. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] applies: independent hosts agree on entity names without coordination.
    • Walkthrough: MaxEntityPathLength = 260 (:14) and MaxSubscriptionNameLength = 50 (:17). Queue(queueName) (:22) and Topic(topic) (:27) fit to 260; Subscription(queueName) (:35) fits the queue name to 50, so each consuming host owns exactly one subscription per topic, named after its queue.
    • Where it's used: AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner, AzureServiceBusPublisher and AzureServiceBusReceiver. The same names are asserted against the deployed infrastructure template by MMCA.ADC/Tests/Services/MMCA.ADC.Services.Tests/Messaging/BrokerTopologyBicepTests.cs, and in Common by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTopologyTests.cs.

    AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner.cs:17 · Level 4 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: creates the entities a host's BrokerTopology needs, idempotently, through the management plane: the host's queue, a topic per consumed event, and on each topic one subscription named after the queue that forwards every message to it. Existing entities are left exactly as they are, and a topic once seen is cached, so a steady-state publish costs no management call (AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner.cs:9-16). Sibling of AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.
    • Depends on: BrokerTopology, AzureServiceBusEntityNames, ServiceBusEmulatorSupport; externals ServiceBusAdministrationClient, ServiceBusException, ConcurrentDictionary.
    • Concept introduced, topic fan-out with auto-forwarding subscriptions. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses the publish/subscribe topology: a publisher only knows a topic per event; each consuming host adds one subscription that auto-forwards into its own queue, so a new consumer never changes a publisher and one queue receives every event the host consumes. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] applies to concurrent startup: an exists-check followed by a create that absorbs MessagingEntityAlreadyExists makes two hosts (or two first publishes) racing for one entity both succeed.
    • Walkthrough:
      • State. The admin client, the isEmulator flag, a logger, and _knownTopics, an ordinal ConcurrentDictionary used as a set (:19-22); constructor at :28.
      • ProvisionAsync (:45-65) returns immediately for a topology with no subscriptions, since a host that only publishes owns no queue (:49-52). It computes the queue and subscription names (:54-55), creates the queue first because a subscription cannot forward to an entity that does not exist yet (:58), then for each distinct topic ensures the topic and its subscription (:60-64).
      • EnsureTopicAsync (:71-86) returns on a cache hit (:73), otherwise checks existence, creates when missing and logs only when this call created it (:78-83), then caches the topic (:85).
      • The option builders are internal static so tests can assert them. BuildTopicOptions (:92) and BuildSubscriptionOptions (:135, ForwardTo at :137) only add the emulator's one-hour DefaultMessageTimeToLive; BuildQueueOptions (:111) also sets DeadLetteringOnMessageExpiration = true and RequiresDuplicateDetection = false (:115-116), the latter because a scheduled redelivery deliberately reuses the original message id.
      • EnsureQueueAsync (:146) and EnsureSubscriptionAsync (:156) follow the same check-then-create shape. CreateUnlessExistsAsync (:171-182) returns true when it created the entity and false on the MessagingEntityAlreadyExists conflict (:178). Logs are event ids 1 and 2 at Information (:184-188).
    • Why it's built this way: provisioning is on by default (MessageBus:ProvisionTopology, MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:84) so a development stack or emulator needs no setup; turning it off lets a deployment own the entities in infrastructure code instead, which the ADC bicep test pins against the same names.
    • Where it's used: registered only when settings.ProvisionTopology is true (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:83-89); called by AzureServiceBusReceiver at startup and by AzureServiceBusPublisher before a first send. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTopologyTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: whether each deployed environment runs with provisioning on or off is configuration, Not determinable from source.

    AzureServiceBusPublisher

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusPublisher.cs:13 · Level 5 · class (internal sealed, IBrokerPublisher, IAsyncDisposable)

    • What it is: the Azure Service Bus IBrokerPublisher: sends each message to its event's topic through one cached ServiceBusSender per topic. A send the broker rejects throws, so the outbox retries the row (AzureServiceBusPublisher.cs:7-12). Sibling of AmazonSqsPublisher.
    • Depends on: IBrokerPublisher, OutgoingBrokerMessage, AzureServiceBusEntityNames, AzureServiceBusMessageMapping, AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner (optional); externals ServiceBusClient, ServiceBusSender.
    • Concept introduced: none new; the outbox's at-least-once publish is taught in the primer (ADR-003). [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] applies: senders are cached per topic so a steady-state publish opens no new link.
    • Walkthrough: fields are the shared client, the optional provisioner and an ordinal ConcurrentDictionary<string, ServiceBusSender> (:15-17); the constructor null-guards only the client (:22). PublishAsync (:31-45) fits the topic name (:35), ensures the topic exists when a provisioner is present (:37-40), gets or creates the sender with a static lambda that captures nothing (:42), and sends the mapped message (:44). DisposeAsync (:48-56) disposes every cached sender and clears the cache.
    • Where it's used: registered as the host's IBrokerPublisher singleton (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:91-93), which the outbox processor uses. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransportTests.cs.

    AzureServiceBusReceiver

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusReceiver.cs:19 · Level 5 · class (internal sealed partial, BackgroundService)

    • What it is: the hosted receiver of the Azure Service Bus transport. In the background, so a broker still warming up or briefly unreachable never fails host startup, it provisions the topology when provisioning is on (retrying with backoff until it succeeds), then runs a ServiceBusProcessor on the host's queue that hands every message to AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler. A host that consumes no event creates no queue and runs no processor (AzureServiceBusReceiver.cs:10-18). Sibling of AmazonSqsReceiver.
    • Depends on: AzureServiceBusConnection, BrokerTopology, AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler, AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner (optional), MessageBusSettings, BrokerTransportPolicies, AzureServiceBusEntityNames; nested ProcessorSettlement. Externals: BackgroundService, ServiceBusProcessor.
    • Concept introduced, lock duration sized to the retry budget. [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] assesses backpressure: concurrency and prefetch come from shared settings, and prefetch defaults to zero on purpose, because a prefetched message's lock runs while it waits and the pipeline can hold a handler for tens of seconds of in-process retry (:24-29). [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] applies to lock renewal: 15 minutes is far beyond the default in-process retry budget (1 + 2 + 4 + 8 + 16 seconds plus handler time), so a message being retried is never handed to a second consumer (:31-36). [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] applies to the log contract: a shutdown cancellation is not reported as an error.
    • Walkthrough:
      • Constants. DefaultMaxConcurrentCalls = 16 (:22), DefaultPrefetchCount = 0 (:29), MaxAutoLockRenewalDuration = 15 minutes (:36), provisioning backoff from 2 seconds to 1 minute (:38-39). The constructor null-guards everything but the optional provisioner (:55-75).
      • BuildProcessorOptions (:80-92) sets PeekLock and AutoCompleteMessages = false (:86-87) so the handler settles explicitly, resolves prefetch and concurrency through BrokerTransportPolicies with the defaults above as fallback (:88-89; settings MessageBusSettings.cs:223, :232), and applies the lock renewal (:90).
      • ExecuteAsync (:95-138) returns for an empty topology (:97-100) and when provisioning is configured but the host stopped first (:104-107). It creates a sender on its own queue for redeliveries (:109) and the processor (:112), wires each message to _handler.HandleAsync with a fresh ProcessorSettlement (:115-116) and errors to OnProcessErrorAsync (:117), starts processing, logs, and waits on an infinite delay until stopping (:121-123). The finally stops the processor if it is still running (:129-134), and the await using blocks dispose processor and sender.
      • ProvisionWithRetryAsync (:145-179) loops: provision, return true on success, return false on a stop-driven cancellation, otherwise log (under a scoped CA1031 suppression at :161) and delay, doubling the backoff up to the one-minute cap (:177).
      • OnProcessErrorAsync (:181-190) logs unless the exception is a cancellation raised while stopping (:184). Log event ids are 20 (Information), 21 and 22 (Error) (:192-199).
    • Why it's built this way: ADR-087 records the receive and poison policies the handler carries out; this class keeps only the Service Bus processor mechanics.
    • Where it's used: registered as a hosted service by AzureServiceBusTransport.Register (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:95-104). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusReceiverTests.cs.

    AzureServiceBusTransport

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Messaging · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:37 · Level 6 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the IBrokerTransport for Azure Service Bus, selected when MessageBus:Provider is AzureServiceBus, built on the Azure.Messaging.ServiceBus SDK alone. It resolves where to connect and registers the connection, the optional provisioner, the publisher and the hosted receiver (AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:37-214).
    • Depends on: IBrokerTransport, BrokerTransportContext (settings, configuration, topology and resolved connection string), IBrokerReceivePipeline, IBrokerPublisher, AzureTokenCredentials, AzureServiceBusTransportSettings, AzureServiceBusEndpoint, AzureServiceBusConnection, AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner, AzureServiceBusPublisher, AzureServiceBusReceiver, AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler, ServiceBusEmulatorSupport. Externals: Azure.Core.TokenCredential, Microsoft.Extensions.DependencyInjection, Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration.
    • Concept introduced, a pluggable broker transport per vendor. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether the broker is a replaceable detail behind the application's messaging: the core picks a transport by name, and this class is the Azure one, living in the vendor assembly beside AmazonSqsTransport. [Rubric §11, Security] applies to sign-in: a configured namespace uses a Microsoft Entra token, so the namespace can run with shared access keys switched off, and that path wins over any connection string. [Rubric §17, DevOps] applies through the emulator branch: a development stack runs the same transport as production.
    • Walkthrough:
      • Identity. ProviderName = "AzureServiceBus" (:40) is returned by Name (:59). FullyQualifiedNamespaceKey composes the configuration key for error messages (:43). MaxRedeliveryDelay is null (:62): Service Bus schedules arbitrarily far ahead, so the shared pipeline applies no cap.
      • Credential factory. The public constructor delegates to AzureTokenCredentials.Create (:48-51); an internal constructor takes any Func<string?, TokenCredential> for tests (:55-56), reached through CreateCredential (:166).
      • Register (:69-105) resolves the endpoint during AddBrokerMessaging, so a configuration error fails startup rather than the first publish (:74-76). It then TryAdds TimeProvider.System (:80), registers AzureServiceBusConnection as a singleton (:81), the provisioner only when settings.ProvisionTopology is on (:83-89), the publisher as IBrokerPublisher (:91-93), and the receiver as a hosted service with a new delivery handler over the host's IBrokerReceivePipeline (:95-104).
      • ResolveEndpoint (:115-161) reads AzureServiceBusTransportSettings (:119). A configured namespace returns a token endpoint with no connection strings (:122-131). With neither namespace nor connection string it throws an InvalidOperationException naming both options and the connection-string keys (:133-140). An emulator connection string returns an endpoint with the derived admin string and isEmulator: true (:145-153). Otherwise the same connection string serves both planes (:155-160).
      • ReadSettings (:171-177) binds MessageBus:AzureServiceBus, falling back to defaults when the section is absent.
      • ResolveNamespaceAddress (:186-204) returns null for blank input, takes the host from an sb:// address (via HostOfServiceBusAddress, :206-210) or trims a trailing slash from a bare host (:194), rejects anything Uri.CheckHostName does not classify as DNS, IPv4 or IPv6 with an error naming the key (:196-201), and returns sb://{host}/ (:203). LoggerFor<T> falls back to NullLoggerFactory when no logger factory is registered (:212-213).
    • Why it's built this way: ADR-066 records transport selection by provider name and dev/prod parity; ADR-087 the redelivery and poison policies, applied unconditionally on Service Bus; ADR-134 the cloud-neutral core with vendor adapter packages, including managed-identity sign-in for this transport; ADR-118 the message-transport library policy. Keeping the vendor transport out of the core keeps the Azure SDK out of hosts that do not use it, which the architecture rules check by listing the Azure namespace root among cloud-vendor dependencies (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture/Rules/Layering/ArchitectureRules.CloudVendors.cs:32).
    • Where it's used: registered by AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.AddAzureServiceBusTransport (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:154-155), documented to be called before AddBrokerMessaging (:139-152). Every MMCA.ADC service host registers it through RegisterBrokerTransports (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:260, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:325, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:140, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:97). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransportTests.cs and AzureServiceBusTransportCredentialTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: which provider and which sign-in path each deployment selects is configuration, Not determinable from source.

    AzureNotificationHubSettings

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubSettings.cs:9 · Level 0 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the options class bound from the NativePush section that switches Azure Notification Hubs delivery on: an Enabled flag, a connection string and a hub name (AzureNotificationHubSettings.cs:9-22).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: configuration as the on switch. [Rubric §17, DevOps] assesses whether a capability can be enabled per environment without a code change: the host registers the channel unconditionally and the section decides (see ADR-044, ADR-070).
    • Walkthrough: SectionName is "NativePush" (:12); Enabled (:15), ConnectionString (:18, documented as a Listen+Send+Manage rule) and HubName (:21) are init-only.
    • Why it's built this way: the registration doc says the hub is provisioned before its FCM/APNs credentials exist, so a deployment needs to hold the section and keep the channel off until the platform credentials are in place (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:76-92).
    • Where it's used: AddAzureNotificationHubsPush binds it (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:99-102) and returns without registering anything unless Enabled is true and both strings are non-blank (:91-96).

    NativePushUserTags

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NativePushUserTags.cs:8 · Level 0 · class (internal static)

    • What it is: the owner-tag vocabulary of the Azure Notification Hubs adapter: how a user id becomes a user:{id} installation tag, and how a set of users becomes the OR tag expressions a send targets (NativePushUserTags.cs:8-29).
    • Depends on: the UserIdentifierType alias; BCL string.Create with CultureInfo.InvariantCulture.
    • Concept introduced, addressing users, not devices. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether an integration hides vendor addressing behind application concepts: callers send to user ids, and this type translates them into the hub's tag language. [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] applies through the chunking: Azure Notification Hubs caps a tag expression at 20 tags, so a large audience becomes several expressions rather than one rejected send (:10-11).
    • Walkthrough: MaxTagsPerExpression = 20 (:11). BuildUserTagExpressions maps every id through UserTag, chunks by 20, and joins each chunk with " || " (:17-21). UserTagPrefix is "user:" (:24), and UserTag formats the id invariantly behind that prefix (:27-28), so the tag stamped at registration and the tag targeted at send time are byte-identical.
    • Why it's built this way: one owner of the tag format keeps the registrar and the sender from drifting apart; a different format on either side would silently deliver to nobody. ADR-044 records the hub as the device registry this tagging relies on.
    • Where it's used: AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender loops BuildUserTagExpressions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:43); AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar stamps UserTag on each installation and uses UserTagPrefix to recognise another owner (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:41, :125-128). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NativePushUserTagsTests.cs.

    NotificationHubConnection

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubConnection.cs:15 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: the parsed NativePush connection string and hub name, plus the Shared Access Signature (SAS) that authorizes every request the framework sends to Azure Notification Hubs (NotificationHubConnection.cs:9-15).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; BCL HMACSHA256, HttpUtility.UrlEncode, UriBuilder and a source-generated Regex (:166-170).
    • Concept introduced, a signed token per request instead of a vendor SDK. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses how a service authenticates to the systems it calls and how long a credential it puts on the wire stays usable. Here the shared access key never leaves the process: each request carries a token signed with it over that request's own audience and valid for twenty minutes (TokenTimeToLive, :21). [Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain] assesses whether a third-party package earns its place. The class reproduces the parsing, the request URI shape and the token algorithm the retired Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK used, so the hub sees the same traffic without the package (:9-14).
    • Walkthrough
      • ApiVersion is "2020-06" (:18); the key is held only as its UTF-8 bytes, set in the private constructor (:27, :29-35). Endpoint, KeyName and HubName are read-only (:38, :41, :44).
      • Parse(connectionString, hubName) (:52-88) splits ";" + connectionString on ;Endpoint=, ;SharedAccessKeyName= and ;SharedAccessKey=, case-insensitive and in any order, so a value runs to the next of the three keys (:57-62, regex at :166-170). An empty value, a repeated key or a missing key throws FormatException (:65-85).
      • CreateRequestUriBuilder (:94-109) forces https, appends {hub}/ and the api-version query; AddToQuery (:115-118) appends a raw, unencoded fragment, which the registrations-by-tag paging relies on.
      • CreateSharedAccessSignature(requestUri, utcNow) (:127-143) normalizes the audience through NormalizeAudience (:145-163: http scheme, no port, query, fragment or credentials, trailing slash), lower-cases and URL-encodes it (:130), sets the expiry in Unix seconds twenty minutes out (:132-134), signs {audience}\n{expiry} with HMAC-SHA256 (:137-140) and returns SharedAccessSignature sr=...&sig=...&se=...&skn=... (:142).
    • Why it's built this way: the clock is a parameter, so the client signs from its TimeProvider and a test can pin the token exactly. The lower-casing is deliberate (the CA1308 suppression at :129 says the hub verifies over the lower-cased audience). ADR-044 is the native push decision this serves.
    • Where it's used: registered as a singleton factory by AddAzureNotificationHubsPush (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:110) and injected into NotificationHubRestClient, which signs every request with it (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:207). Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubConnectionTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the singleton is a factory registration, so a malformed connection string throws when the REST client is first resolved, not while the host builds its service collection.

    NotificationHubException

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubContracts.cs:69 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the one failure type the hub client raises: a non-success status or a transport failure, classified as transient or permanent, with the hub's requested wait and a not-found flag (NotificationHubContracts.cs:63-91).
    • Depends on: BCL Exception and HttpStatusCode.
    • Concept introduced, classify a failure once, where it is observed. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses whether a system tells a failure worth retrying from one that is not. The classification happens when the response is translated (NotificationHubWireFormat), and everything downstream reads the flags instead of re-inspecting status codes: the retry policy reads IsTransient and RetryAfter, the registrar reads IsEntityNotFound.
    • Walkthrough: the internal constructor takes the message, status, transient flag, optional wait and inner exception (:72-78). StatusCode is null for a transport failure (:81); IsTransient (:84); RetryAfter (:87); IsEntityNotFound is true for a 404 or a 204 where content was expected (:90). The pragma at :68 suppresses the standard-constructor rules because the type is internal and only the client raises it.
    • Where it's used: created by NotificationHubWireFormat (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:171-212), classified by NotificationHubRetryPolicy, and caught by AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:69, :99, :104, :126, :204). AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender catches any exception, this one included.

    NotificationHubInstallationState

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubContracts.cs:26 · Level 0 · record (internal sealed)

    • What it is: an installation as the hub returns it, reduced to the two members the registrar reads: PushChannel and Tags (NotificationHubContracts.cs:25-26).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: the read-side twin of NotificationHubInstallation. Reading only what the ownership checks need keeps the parser small and tolerant of members the hub adds later.

    NotificationHubNotification

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubContracts.cs:32 · Level 0 · record (internal sealed)

    • What it is: one native notification: the ServiceBusNotification-Format value that picks the platform and that platform's JSON payload (NotificationHubContracts.cs:28-52).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Walkthrough: the private constructor rejects a blank body (:34-39), so the only way in is the two factories. Format (:42) and Body (:45, sent as application/json). FcmV1(json) uses format fcmv1 (:48); Apple(json) uses format apple (:51). Note the send format for Apple is apple, while the installation platform for the same devices is apns (see NotificationHubPlatform).
    • Where it's used: built by AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender once per chunk per platform (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:45, :56); NotificationHubRestClient writes Format into the header and Body into the content (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:190-191).

    NotificationHubPlatform

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubContracts.cs:6 · Level 0 · enum (internal)

    • What it is: the two platforms an installation can target: FcmV1 (Firebase Cloud Messaging HTTP v1, Android, :9) and Apns (Apple Push Notification service, :12).
    • Where it's used: AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar maps the request's platform string onto it (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:28-33), and NotificationHubWireFormat writes it as fcmV1 or apns, throwing on any other value (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:52-57).

    NotificationHubRegistrationPage

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubContracts.cs:59 · Level 0 · record (internal sealed)
