    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubContracts.cs:19 · Level 1 · record (internal sealed)

    • What it is: a hub installation as the registrar writes it: the client-owned InstallationId, the NotificationHubPlatform, the device's PushChannel and the Tags (NotificationHubContracts.cs:15-23).
    • Depends on: NotificationHubPlatform.
    • Concept: the write-side shape of the installation model ADR-044 adopts; its read-side twin is NotificationHubInstallationState.
    • Where it's used: assembled by AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar with exactly one tag, the owner stamp (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:42-46); NotificationHubRestClient rejects a blank id with InvalidOperationException (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:82-85) and sends the JSON NotificationHubWireFormat.WriteInstallation produces (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:44-77).

    NotificationHubRetryPolicy

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRetryPolicy.cs:14 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the hub client's retry: up to three retries of a transient failure with exponential backoff (2, 4, 8 seconds plus up to 8 percent jitter) capped at one minute, or the hub's own Retry-After wait when it sends one (NotificationHubRetryPolicy.cs:6-14).
    • Depends on: NotificationHubException for the classification; BCL TimeProvider, ExceptionDispatchInfo, SocketException, TimeoutException.
    • Concept introduced, retry what can succeed, wait as long as the server asks. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses whether a dependency's brownout is absorbed without amplifying it. The policy retries only failures classified transient, honors the hub's own wait, caps every wait, and never retries the caller's cancellation. The composition removes the standard resilience handlers from the typed client (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:121-132), so this is the only retry layer and the two cannot multiply each other's attempts. [Rubric §14, Testability] applies through the TimeProvider parameter: the waits run on an injected clock.
    • Walkthrough
      • JitterFactor is 0.08 (:16); the constructor takes the retry count, base delay and cap (:18-23); Default is 3 retries, one second, one minute (:26).
      • RunAsync(operation, timeProvider, ct) (:40-67) runs the operation, which builds a fresh request each time (new tracking id and token). On a failure it asks CalculateRetryDelay, then either waits through Task.Delay(delay, timeProvider, ct) (:57) or rethrows the original exception with its stack through ExceptionDispatchInfo (:61). A token cancelled between attempts ends the loop with TaskCanceledException (:66).
      • CalculateRetryDelay(exception, attemptCount) (:70-88) returns null when retries are disabled, exhausted or the failure is not retryable (:72-79); otherwise the hub's RetryAfter, or 2^n times the base delay plus jitter (:82-84), capped at MaxDelay (:87).
      • ShouldRetry (:95-110) unwraps an OperationCanceledException to its inner exception (an attempt that hit the client timeout carries a TimeoutException, the caller's own cancellation carries none) and flattens an AggregateException (:97-102); a transient hub failure, a TimeoutException or a SocketException retries (:104-109).
    • Why it's built this way: these are the defaults the Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK ran with, so replacing the package did not change how a brownout is weathered (:10-12).
    • Where it's used: Default is passed to the client at registration (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:116); NotificationHubRestClient wraps every operation in it except the registrations-by-tag read. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubConnectionTests.cs.

    INotificationHubRestClient

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:14 · Level 2 · interface (internal)

    • What it is: the five Azure Notification Hubs REST operations native push uses, with every failure surfacing as a NotificationHubException (NotificationHubRestClient.cs:8-30).
    • Depends on: NotificationHubInstallation, NotificationHubInstallationState, NotificationHubRegistrationPage, NotificationHubNotification.
    • Concept: [Rubric §14, Testability] assesses whether a component can be exercised without its external dependency. The sender and registrar take this interface, not an HttpClient, so their tests run against MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/FakeNotificationHubRestClient.cs, while the HTTP shape is pinned separately against the concrete client.
    • Walkthrough: CreateOrUpdateInstallationAsync is PUT installations/{id}, a full overwrite (:17); GetInstallationAsync is GET installations/{id}, a 404 throwing a not-found failure (:20); DeleteInstallationAsync is DELETE installations/{id} (:23); GetRegistrationsByTagAsync reads one page of GET tags/{tag}/registrations (:26); SendNotificationAsync is POST messages with a tag expression (:29).
    • Where it's used: implemented by NotificationHubRestClient and registered as a typed HttpClient (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:111); consumed by AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender and AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.

    NotificationHubWireFormat

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:18 · Level 2 · class (internal static partial)

    • What it is: the bodies the hub exchanges: the installation JSON written on upsert and read back on get, the Atom feed of registrations, and the translation of a failed response into a NotificationHubException (NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:11-18).
    • Depends on: NotificationHubInstallation, NotificationHubInstallationState, NotificationHubPlatform, NotificationHubException; BCL JsonDocument, XmlReader and source-generated Regex.
    • Concept introduced, replacing an SDK without changing a byte on the wire. [Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain] assesses whether removing a dependency is done safely. Every shape here matches what the 4.2.0 SDK wrote and accepted, property order, null handling and string escaping included (:14-16), so the hub sees identical requests. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] applies through the failure translation, which is where transient and permanent are decided.
    • Walkthrough
      • WriteInstallation (:44-77) hand-writes the JSON in the SDK's property order, with userId, pushChannelExpired and expirationTime as explicit nulls and tags only when set; the platform maps to fcmV1 or apns and anything else throws (:52-57). AppendString (:219-245) escapes as the SDK's serializer did: quote, backslash and control characters (table built at :248-264) plus U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029.
      • ReadInstallation (:84-118) treats a blank body or JSON null as no installation, throws JsonException for a non-object, and reads pushChannel and tags case-insensitively (:105-115).
      • ReadRegistrationTagsAsync (:139-163) requires a feed root (:145-148) and reads each entry's Tags through ReadDescriptionTagsAsync (:282-303). ParseTags (:306-320) applies the hub's tag rules: a string with a malformed tag, more than one $InstallationId or $UserId system tag, or a tag over MaximumTagSize (120, :24) reads as no tags; the regexes are at :349-357.
      • TranslateFailureAsync (:171-186) builds a message with the status, the <Error><Detail> text (ReadErrorDetail, :323-347) and the TrackingId. A 429, or a 403 carrying Retry-After, is transient with the hub's wait or DefaultThrottleWait of ten seconds (:34, :180-183); otherwise only 500, 503, 504 and 408 are transient (IsServerBusy, :189-193).
      • TranslateTransportFailure (:199-212) marks a socket error transient unless it names an unreachable or refused host, and everything else permanent, with a null status.
    • Why it's built this way: keeping every body in one static class lets MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClientRequestShapeTests.cs pin the exact request shapes the SDK sent (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:32-39).
    • Where it's used: only by NotificationHubRestClient.

    AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:25 · Level 3 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: the Azure implementation of INativePushSender, sending both the FCM v1 and the APNs payload for every notification to a set of users (resolved by tag) through the framework's own Notification Hubs REST client, and reporting the result as a NativePushOutcome counted in batches (AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:7-27).

    • Depends on: INotificationHubRestClient (the hub's REST operations over HttpClient, with no Notification Hubs SDK) and ILogger<T> as its two primary-constructor parameters (AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:25-27), NotificationHubNotification for the two platform sends, NativePushPayloads for the payloads, NativePushUserTags for the tag expressions, and INativePushSender as the contract.

    • Concept introduced, one logical notification, two platform sends. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether a capability that varies by platform is normalized behind one application-facing call. A caller says "notify these users, with this title and body"; it does not know or care which of them are on iOS. This class turns that into two hub calls per audience chunk, one per platform, and the hub decides which installations each applies to. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] applies through the delivery posture in the class doc: every chunk is attempted on every platform, a failing send does not stop the later chunks or the other platform, and a failure is counted and returned rather than thrown (:12-18), which is the side-effect contract of ADR-096. A push that fails does not fail the command that triggered it.

    • Walkthrough

      • SendToUsersAsync (AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:33-70) builds each payload once (:35-36), then loops the tag expressions produced by NativePushUserTags (:43), sending a NotificationHubNotification.FcmV1 and a NotificationHubNotification.Apple per chunk (:45, :56) and counting each platform's sent and failed batches. For an audience of 45 users that is 3 chunks and 6 hub calls, and the payload serialization happened twice in total.
      • TrySendAsync (:74-87) wraps one send: success returns null; any exception other than the caller's own cancellation is logged at Warning with the platform and its type name (declared at :92-93) and returned, so it is counted and the loop continues (:81-86). The caller's cancellation propagates. A send reaches this catch only after NotificationHubRetryPolicy inside the client has given up on it.
      • The outcome is NativePushOutcome.From(NativePushDeliveryUnit.Batch, ...) with the first failure attached (:69), after one source-generated Information log carrying the title and the per-platform batch counts (:68, declared at :89-90). The wording is precise: the notification was handed to the hub. Actual delivery to a device is asynchronous and outside this process.
      • Every await uses ConfigureAwait(false), which is the library policy of ADR-049.

    • Why it's built this way: sending the two payloads unconditionally rather than looking up each user's platform keeps the sender free of any device registry of its own. The hub already knows each installation's platform from the registration written by AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar, so a payload that does not apply to a given installation is simply not delivered to it. That is the division of labour ADR-044 records: the hub is the device registry, and this codebase stores no push tokens. NotificationHubRestClient issues the same requests the Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK did, request for request and with its retry policy (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:32-39), so the HTTP traffic is unchanged. ADR-134 records why it lives in the vendor adapter assembly rather than the core.

    • Where it's used: registered only by AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.AddAzureNotificationHubsPush (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:133), and only when AzureNotificationHubSettings has Enabled: true, a connection string and a hub name (:102-107); the REST client is registered as a typed HttpClient in the same call, with its factory loggers and standard resilience handlers removed (:110-132). The type is internal, so a host resolves INativePushSender, never this class. The consuming application code is SendPushNotificationHandler.

    • Caveats / not-in-source: whether a given deployment has working platform credentials is a provisioning question, Not determinable from source.

    NotificationHubRestClient

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:40 · Level 3 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the INotificationHubRestClient implementation over a factory HttpClient, authorized with a Shared Access Signature per request, whose request shapes, accepted statuses, failure classification and retry are those of the retired Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK (NotificationHubRestClient.cs:32-40).
    • Depends on: NotificationHubConnection for the URIs and tokens, NotificationHubRetryPolicy, NotificationHubWireFormat for bodies and failures, and the contract records (NotificationHubInstallation, NotificationHubInstallationState, NotificationHubRegistrationPage, NotificationHubNotification); BCL HttpClient and TimeProvider.
    • Concept introduced, owning the vendor protocol in a few hundred lines. [Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain] assesses whether a dependency is worth what it costs. Native push needs five REST calls, so the framework issues them itself rather than carrying an SDK, and pins the traffic with request-shape tests so the swap is observable. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] applies through the per-request TrackingId header, which every failure message repeats so a hub-side trace can be matched, and through the composition's decision to remove the factory loggers because the hub's URIs carry user tags and installation ids (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:117-120).
    • Walkthrough
      • OperationTimeout is 60 seconds per attempt (:43); UserAgent is the SDK's NHub/2020-06 (api-origin=DotNetSdk;...) string (:46-47); the accepted statuses are 200, 204, or 200 and 201 (:51-53). The constructor sets the timeout and adds the User-Agent on the fresh factory client (:64-76).
      • CreateOrUpdateInstallationAsync (:79-101) rejects a blank id with InvalidOperationException (:82-85) and PUTs the JSON from WriteInstallation, accepting only 200. GetInstallationAsync (:104-121) reads it back through ReadInstallation; DeleteInstallationAsync (:124-140) accepts only 204.
      • GetRegistrationsByTagAsync (:143-171) appends $top and the continuation token as raw query fragments (:149-158) and builds the page from the Atom feed and the x-ms-continuationtoken header (:165-169). It is the one operation sent once, without the retry policy, as the SDK did (:37-38).
      • SendNotificationAsync (:174-199) POSTs to messages with ServiceBusNotification-Tags when a tag expression is given (:185-188) and ServiceBusNotification-Format from the notification (:190), accepting 200 or 201.
      • CreateRequest (:201-209) gives every attempt a new GUID TrackingId and a freshly signed Authorization header from the injected clock. SendAsync (:211-232) maps an HttpRequestException through TranslateTransportFailure (:218-221) and any unaccepted status through TranslateFailureAsync (:228-231).
    • Why it's built this way: the retry lives in NotificationHubRetryPolicy alone because the composition strips the standard resilience handlers from this typed client (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:121-132), so attempts are not multiplied by two stacked policies. ADR-044 and ADR-134 place it in the Azure adapter assembly.
    • Where it's used: registered by AddHttpClient<INotificationHubRestClient, NotificationHubRestClient> with TimeProvider from DI or TimeProvider.System and NotificationHubRetryPolicy.Default (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:111-116); reached through the interface by AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender and AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClientRequestShapeTests.cs.

    AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:15 · Level 4 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the Azure implementation of IPushDeviceRegistrar. It maintains device installations in Azure Notification Hubs using the installation model (client-generated stable ids, full upsert semantics) and stamps each installation with its owner's user:{id} tag so sends can target users rather than raw device tokens (AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:8-225).

    • Depends on: INotificationHubRestClient (whose failures all surface as NotificationHubException, not-found flagged by IsEntityNotFound) and ILogger<T> as its two primary-constructor parameters (AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:15-17), NotificationHubInstallation, NotificationHubInstallationState and NotificationHubPlatform as the hub shapes, NativePushUserTags for the owner tag, DeviceInstallationRequest as the input shape, and Result / Error.

    • Concept introduced, the hub as the only ownership store, and the existence oracle it must not become. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether an endpoint can be used to learn something the caller is not entitled to know, and [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] assesses whether the result shape is honest about what happened. Installation ids are client-generated, so nothing stops a caller from sending someone else's, and the upsert is a full overwrite that would re-tag and re-point another user's device. The framework stores no device table of its own, so both checks run against the hub: the delete is a read-then-delete comparing the owner tag (AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:88-96), and the upsert first asks ClaimedByAnotherDeviceAsync (:197-215). The installation id is per device, so a second user signing in on the same phone legitimately presents an id the previous user owns; when the request also carries the installation's current push channel the caller holds the device and the installation is handed over (re-tagged, so the previous owner's pushes stop). Without the matching channel the upsert answers with a conflict, which reveals nothing exploitable because ids are random client GUIDs, and the client recovers by minting a fresh id (:50-64). The delete path reports a mismatch as success, not as not-found; the interface remarks explain why (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Notifications/IPushDeviceRegistrar.cs:46): answering differently for "no such installation" and "not yours" would turn the endpoint into an existence oracle for other users' installation ids.

    • Walkthrough

      • Two constants frame the class: InstallationIdTagPrefix is "$InstallationId:{" (AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:20) and RegistrationPageSize is 100, the hub's page cap (:23).
      • UpsertAsync(userId, request, ct) (:26-77) starts with the platform switch: FCMV1 and APNS, compared after ToUpperInvariant, map to NotificationHubPlatform.FcmV1 and .Apns, and anything else yields null (:28-33).
      • An unmapped platform returns a validation failure with code PushDevice.UnsupportedPlatform, and the message quotes the two accepted constants from DeviceInstallationRequest (:34-40).
      • The NotificationHubInstallation is assembled with the client-supplied id, the resolved platform, the push channel, and exactly one tag, the owner stamp from NativePushUserTags.UserTag (:42-46). Before writing, the upsert calls ClaimedByAnotherDeviceAsync; when it returns true the result is Error.Conflict with code PushDevice.InstallationConflict (:58-64). CreateOrUpdateInstallationAsync is a full upsert, so re-registering the same id replaces the record rather than accumulating duplicates (:66).
      • ClaimedByAnotherDeviceAsync (:197-215) is true only when all three hold: the installation exists (a not-found NotificationHubException on the read means free, :204-207), it carries a user:-prefixed tag that is not the caller's own (:209-212), and its stored push channel differs from the request's, compared ordinally (:213-214).
      • The upsert's catch is narrow (NotificationHubException) and translates to PushDevice.UpsertFailed after logging the real exception (:69-76).
      • DeleteAsync(userId, installationId, ct) (:80-109) reads the installation, checks ownership, and deletes only on a match (:88-96). The comment at :84-87 settles the concurrency question: a concurrent re-registration of the same id between the read and the delete is the owner's own doing, so no lock is warranted.
      • Two catches follow. A not-found NotificationHubException returns success, because an unknown installation is already the desired state (:99-103); any other NotificationHubException logs and returns PushDevice.DeleteFailed (:104-108).
      • DeleteAllForUserAsync (:119-143) finds the user's installations through the registrations the hub materializes for them: it pages the registrations tagged with the owner tag (FindInstallationIdsAsync, :150-173), reads each one's $InstallationId:{id} system tag (TryReadInstallationId, :176-189), and sends every id through DeleteAsync, which re-checks ownership (remarks at :112-118).
      • OwnedBy (:217-218) checks the tags contain the owner tag with StringComparer.Ordinal. DeleteFailed (:220-224) is factored out so the failure shape is written once.

    • Why it's built this way: ADR-044 puts the device registry in the hub rather than in the application database, which keeps push tokens (a credential-shaped secret) out of the application's storage entirely. The cost of that choice is the read-then-delete ownership check here, and the interface remarks accept it explicitly.

    • Where it's used: registered by AddAzureNotificationHubsPush alongside the sender (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:134); the type is internal, so a host resolves IPushDeviceRegistrar. Called from the framework's DevicesController, which lets a client register a device without any application-specific endpoint. The AWS and GCP siblings are SnsPushDeviceRegistrar and FcmPushDeviceRegistrar. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrarTests.cs.

    • Caveats / not-in-source: an installation registered before ownership tagging carries no user: tag, and the delete path treats it exactly like someone else's (success, no delete), as the comment at :91-92 says. On the upsert side such an installation is claimable by any caller, because it has no other user's owner tag. Nothing in this code cleans up such records.

    AzureBlobFileStorageSettings

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Storage · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Storage/AzureBlobFileStorageSettings.cs:10 · Level 0 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the options class bound from the FileStorage section for the Azure Blob adapter: a blob service endpoint, a connection-string alternative, and the one container every blob lives in (AzureBlobFileStorageSettings.cs:10-23).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; System.Uri only.
    • Concept: a plain bound-options class; the fail-fast configuration contract it participates in is recorded in ADR-070. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether production authenticates with an identity rather than a shared key: ServiceUri is the managed-identity path (DefaultAzureCredential), and ConnectionString is documented as the local-development (Azurite) alternative (:15-19).
    • Walkthrough: SectionName is "FileStorage" (:13); ServiceUri (:16), ConnectionString (:19) and ContainerName (:22, documented as required) are init-only.
    • Why it's built this way: one section with two mutually-exclusive credential shapes lets the same host code run against Azurite locally and a managed identity in Azure. It shares the FileStorage section name with S3FileStorageSettings, so a host picks its vendor by which registration it calls, not by renaming configuration (ADR-045).
    • Where it's used: AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.AddAzureBlobFileStorage binds it (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:48-51) and registers nothing when ContainerName is blank or neither an absolute ServiceUri nor a ConnectionString is present (:49-59); the comment at :54 records the trap that an empty-string ServiceUri binds to a relative Uri, so only IsAbsoluteUri counts.

    AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.PostgreSql · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:10 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the bound options for Microsoft Entra token login to Azure Database for PostgreSQL, section AzurePostgreSql:EntraAuthentication (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:13).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: cross-reference AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator, which introduces the Azure form of token-as-password database authentication.
    • Walkthrough: a single member, Enabled (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:16), off when absent. The class doc records why it needs nothing else: the database user is the connection string's Username (the Entra principal name), and the identity whose token is sent is whatever DefaultAzureCredential resolves to, a user-assigned managed identity being selected through AZURE_CLIENT_ID (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:3-9). It mirrors the GCP sibling CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings.
    • Why it's built this way: an explicit boolean gate switches PostgreSQL onto Entra tokens by configuration alone, and an absent or false value makes registration a no-op (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:185-191). See ADR-070 and ADR-134.
    • Where it's used: bound in AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication of AzureServiceCollectionExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:186-187); SectionPath is quoted in the TLS error of AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:103).

    • What it is: the bound options for Microsoft Entra token login to Azure Cache for Redis and Azure Managed Redis, section AzureRedis:EntraAuthentication (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:13).

    GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:14 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: an IHostedService that logs one startup Warning when the shared DataProtection key ring is persisted to Cloud Storage with no DataProtection:GcpKmsKeyName, in a host that is not Development, because the key ring XML then sits in the object without an at-rest key (GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:6-11).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; ILogger<T> as its one primary-constructor parameter and IHostedService (GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:14-15).
    • Concept: cross-reference GcpKmsXmlEncryptor, which introduces key-ring encryption at rest; this type is the visible signal when that encryptor is not configured. The Azure sibling is AzureKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.
    • Walkthrough: KmsKeyNameKey is "DataProtection:GcpKmsKeyName" (GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:18). StartAsync logs the notice once and completes (GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:21-25); StopAsync does nothing (GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:28). The message is a source-generated [LoggerMessage] at LogLevel.Warning that names the configuration key to set (GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:30-33).
    • Why it's built this way: a missing KMS key is a warning, not a startup failure, for the reason the Azure sibling gives (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:118-119). It is registered only in the case it reports, so a host that configured the key, or runs in Development, logs nothing (GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:9-11). The key constant lives here and the registration reuses it, so the notice and the setting cannot name different keys (GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:25). See ADR-069.
    • Where it's used: added with AddHostedService by GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.AddGoogleCloudDataProtection when no KMS key name was parsed and the environment is not Development (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:112-121).

    GcpKmsXmlDecryptor

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:16 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the ASP.NET Core DataProtection IXmlDecryptor that reverses GcpKmsXmlEncryptor, decrypting a stored key element with the Cloud KMS key that element names (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:10-15).
    • Depends on: GcpKmsXmlEncryptor for the element-name constants; Google KeyManagementServiceClient, DecryptRequest, ByteString; IServiceProvider.
    • Concept: cross-reference GcpKmsXmlEncryptor for the envelope format. The decryptor half of a DataProtection pair is activated by type: the encryptor records the decryptor's type in the key ring, and DataProtection later instantiates it through an IServiceProvider constructor (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:12-14).
    • Walkthrough: _client (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:18) is resolved in the IServiceProvider constructor with GetRequiredService<KeyManagementServiceClient>() (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:22-25). Decrypt(encryptedElement) (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:29) reads the kmsKeyName and value children (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:33-34) and throws InvalidOperationException naming both elements when either is blank (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:35-39). It then sends a synchronous DecryptRequest with the named key and the base64-decoded ciphertext (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:41-45) and parses the UTF-8 plaintext back into an XElement (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:47).
    • Why it's built this way: the key name is read from the element rather than from configuration, so a ring encrypted under one key keeps decrypting regardless of current settings. Taking the client from the container means it is the one AddGoogleCloudDataProtection registers or one the host registered first (GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:14-15); see GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.
    • Where it's used: named as the decryptor type in GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.Encrypt (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:68); exercised by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptionTests.cs.

    GcpKmsXmlEncryptor

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:25 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the DataProtection IXmlEncryptor that encrypts each key element with a Cloud KMS symmetric key before it reaches the key ring, so the stored object holds only ciphertext (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:9-13).
    • Depends on: GcpKmsXmlDecryptor (named in the result); Google KeyManagementServiceClient, CryptoKeyName, EncryptRequest, ByteString; DataProtection EncryptedXmlInfo.
    • Concept introduced, key-ring encryption at rest with a cloud KMS. DataProtection lets a host plug an IXmlEncryptor between key generation and the repository; the encryptor returns the stored form plus the decryptor type that can reverse it. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses protection of secrets at rest; here reading a key needs cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToDecrypt on top of read access to the bucket (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:11-12), so a leaked bucket alone does not leak the keys.
    • Walkthrough: three element-name constants, encryptedKey, kmsKeyName and value (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:28,31,34). The internal constructor takes the client and a CryptoKeyName, null-checking both (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:41-45); KeyName exposes the key (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:48). Encrypt(plaintextElement) (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:51) serializes the element with SaveOptions.DisableFormatting to UTF-8 (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:55), sends a synchronous EncryptRequest (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:56-60), builds <encryptedKey> with an explanatory comment, the key name and the base64 ciphertext (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:62-66), and returns EncryptedXmlInfo(encrypted, typeof(GcpKmsXmlDecryptor)) (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:68).
    • Why it's built this way: the element is sent to KMS whole because a key element is about a kilobyte, far under the 64 KiB plaintext limit (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:12-13), so no local envelope key is needed. The ciphertext records the key version, so rotating the KMS key needs no code change and old keys keep decrypting while their version is enabled (GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:21-22).
    • Where it's used: constructed by ProtectKeysWithGoogleCloudKms in GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:59); its constants are read by GcpKmsXmlDecryptor; exercised by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptionTests.cs and MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs.

    GcsObjectLocation

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcsObjectLocation.cs:11 · Level 0 · record (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the Cloud Storage object that holds the DataProtection key ring, a positional record of Bucket and ObjectName parsed from the gs://bucket/object form of DataProtection:GcsObjectUri (GcsObjectLocation.cs:3-11).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: a small parse-once value that fails loudly on a malformed setting, the fail-fast configuration contract of ADR-070.
    • Walkthrough: Scheme is gs:// (GcsObjectLocation.cs:13). Parse(value, key) (GcsObjectLocation.cs:23) matches the scheme case-insensitively (GcsObjectLocation.cs:27), splits the remainder at the first / (GcsObjectLocation.cs:29-30), and accepts it only when the bucket is non-empty, the object name is non-empty and the value does not end in / (GcsObjectLocation.cs:31-33); anything else throws InvalidOperationException naming the configuration key and the bad value (GcsObjectLocation.cs:37-38). ToString() reassembles gs://bucket/object (GcsObjectLocation.cs:42).
    • Why it's built this way: parsed by hand rather than through Uri, which lower-cases and re-escapes an unknown scheme's authority and path in ways a bucket or object name must not be (GcsObjectLocation.cs:5-7). A trailing / is rejected because it names a folder, not an object (GcsObjectLocation.cs:16-17).
    • Where it's used: parsed in GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions before anything is registered (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:91), passed through PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34) into GcsXmlRepository; exercised by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcsObjectLocationTests.cs.

    AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.PostgreSql · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:12 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the default token source behind AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator: it returns a Microsoft Entra access token for the Azure Database for PostgreSQL scope (AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:5-10).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; Azure.Core.TokenCredential and TokenRequestContext.
    • Concept: cross-reference AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator for token-as-password; the GCP counterpart is CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.
    • Walkthrough: Scope is https://ossrdbms-aad.database.windows.net/.default (AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:15). The primary constructor takes a TokenCredential, null-checked into _credential (AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:12,17). GetTokenAsync(cancellationToken) (AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:22) requests a token for that one scope and returns its string, sent to the server as the password (AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:24-27).
    • Why it's built this way: the credential is created with the configurator but no token is requested until the first connection, and the credential caches and refreshes its own token, so each call is an in-memory read until the token nears expiry (AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:7-9). Unlike the GCP sibling it needs no lazy-load field, because the credential object is passed in rather than loaded. See ADR-134.
    • Where it's used: the public constructor of AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator builds it over new DefaultAzureCredential() and binds GetTokenAsync as the token delegate (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35); referenced by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationTests.cs.

    AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Redis · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:21 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: an IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator that puts every framework Redis connection on Microsoft Entra token login through Microsoft.Azure.StackExchangeRedis: the user and password become the identity's object id and an Entra access token, and the library re-authenticates each open connection before the current token expires (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:7-13).
    • Depends on: IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator, AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings (for SectionPath in its messages); externals Azure.Core.TokenCredential, StackExchange.Redis ConfigurationOptions and EndPointCollection, and the library's ConfigureForAzureWithTokenCredentialAsync.
    • Concept introduced, bearer-token login on a cache connection. The token-as-password pattern of AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator applied to Redis, where the access key would otherwise be the shared secret. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses credential handling: no access key is needed, and because the token is a bearer credential sent as the password, a connection without TLS or one that still carries an access key is rejected rather than connected (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:11-13). [Rubric §14, Testability] also applies: an internal constructor takes the configuration step as a delegate.
    • Walkthrough: the public constructor takes a TokenCredential and chains to CreateConfigureStep (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:27-28), which null-checks it and returns options => options.ConfigureForAzureWithTokenCredentialAsync(credential) (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:64-67); the internal constructor accepts any Func<ConfigurationOptions, Task> for tests (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35). Configure(options) (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:41) first throws when the connection string carries a password, because an explicit password takes precedence over the token and the connection would keep signing in with the access key (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:45-50). It then runs the library step synchronously under a VSTHRD002 suppression (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:52-54), and afterwards throws when options.Ssl is false (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:56-61). Both messages name the endpoints through Describe, which prints (no endpoint) for an empty list (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:70-73).
    • Why it's built this way: Configure runs inside the options pipeline, which is synchronous, while the library step is asynchronous because it acquires the first token before returning; the wait runs once per framework connection point before that connection opens, and every point shares one credential, so only the first goes over the network (AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:15-20). Registration also adds a HostIntegrationMarker receipt the core startup tripwire checks, so setting the gate without the call fails the host instead of signing in with the access key (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:242-250). See ADR-134.
    • Where it's used: registered by AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication in AzureServiceCollectionExtensions over a credential from AzureTokenCredentials.Create(settings.ManagedIdentityClientId) (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:252-253); the registration doc names the connection points it covers: the Redis client and distributed cache, the output-cache store, the SignalR backplane and the PING health check's connection (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:211-212). Exercised by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationTests.cs.

    CloudSqlAccessTokenSource

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.CloudSql · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:12 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the default token source behind CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator: it returns an OAuth2 access token for the Cloud SQL login scope from Application Default Credentials (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:5-10).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; Google GoogleCredential and ITokenAccess (Google.Apis.Auth.OAuth2).
    • Concept: cross-reference CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator, which introduces the GCP form of token-as-password database authentication (the AWS form is RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator).
    • Walkthrough: Scope is https://www.googleapis.com/auth/sqlservice.login (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:15). _credential (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:17) caches the scoped credential. GetTokenAsync(cancellationToken) (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:22) reads the cached field, and when it is null loads GoogleCredential.GetApplicationDefaultAsync and calls CreateScoped(Scope) (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:27-32), then returns GetAccessTokenForRequestAsync(authUri: null, ...) (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:35).
    • Why it's built this way: the credential is loaded on the first request rather than at registration, so a host builds its container without Google credentials and only the first database connection needs them (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:7-9). The credential caches and refreshes its own token, so each call is an in-memory read until expiry nears (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:9-10). The lazy load is deliberately unlocked: a race between two first callers loads the credential twice and keeps the last, and a failed load is not cached, so the next call (after Npgsql's failure interval) tries again (CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:24-26). See ADR-113.
    • Where it's used: the public constructor of CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator binds its GetTokenAsync as the token delegate (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35); referenced by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationTests.cs.

    GcsXmlRepository

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcsXmlRepository.cs:31 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: a DataProtection IXmlRepository that keeps the whole key ring as one Cloud Storage object, a <repository> document whose children are the key elements, the same shape the Azure Blob repository uses (GcsXmlRepository.cs:12-15).
    • Depends on: GcsObjectLocation; Google StorageClient, GoogleApiException, UploadObjectOptions, DownloadObjectOptions; System.Xml.Linq.
    • Concept introduced, optimistic concurrency on an object store. Writes are read-modify-write under an object generation precondition, the Cloud Storage counterpart of an ETag: the upload carries IfGenerationMatch set to the generation it read (0 when the object did not exist, which lets the write only create it), so two replicas adding a key at once cannot overwrite each other (GcsXmlRepository.cs:17-22). [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses whether scale-out keeps sessions valid; one shared, lost-update-free ring is what lets every replica unprotect the others' cookies and antiforgery tokens. [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] also applies: concurrent writers converge by retry rather than by a lock.
    • Walkthrough: MaxAttempts is 10 (GcsXmlRepository.cs:34); the root element is repository (GcsXmlRepository.cs:36); WriterSettings emit UTF-8 without a byte order mark (GcsXmlRepository.cs:39). The constructor takes the client, the location and a retryDelay backoff unit (GcsXmlRepository.cs:48-53). GetAllElements() returns the root's children from a fresh read (GcsXmlRepository.cs:59-60). StoreElement(element, friendlyName) (GcsXmlRepository.cs:63) loops up to MaxAttempts (GcsXmlRepository.cs:67): it reads the document and its generation (GcsXmlRepository.cs:69), appends a copy of the element so the caller's element is never re-parented into a discarded document (GcsXmlRepository.cs:71-72), serializes to a stream (GcsXmlRepository.cs:74-80), and uploads with IfGenerationMatch = generation (GcsXmlRepository.cs:84-88). A 412 PreconditionFailed means another writer won, so it sleeps retryDelay * attempt (linear backoff) and re-reads the winner's ring (GcsXmlRepository.cs:90-97). After the last attempt it throws naming the location and the attempt count (GcsXmlRepository.cs:101-102). The private Read() (GcsXmlRepository.cs:105) fetches metadata, returns an empty ring at generation 0 on 404 (GcsXmlRepository.cs:114-119), downloads pinned to the metadata's generation (GcsXmlRepository.cs:125-129), and loops when that generation vanished between the two calls (GcsXmlRepository.cs:131-134), returning the parsed document and generation (GcsXmlRepository.cs:138).
    • Why it's built this way: pinning the download to the generation the metadata reported guarantees the generation a write is conditioned on is the one whose content it extended (GcsXmlRepository.cs:22-23). IXmlRepository is synchronous and the storage client has synchronous methods, so nothing blocks on a task; DataProtection reads the ring rarely and writes only when it creates a key (GcsXmlRepository.cs:25-28). See ADR-069; the Azure and AWS counterparts are AzureDataProtectionExtensions and AwsDataProtectionExtensions.
    • Where it's used: constructed by PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage in GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:38); exercised by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcsXmlRepositoryTests.cs and MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the friendlyName parameter is not used by this implementation (GcsXmlRepository.cs:63-102).

    AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.PostgreSql · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23 · Level 2 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: an INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator that puts every PostgreSQL data source on Microsoft Entra token login for Azure Database for PostgreSQL: the password becomes an Entra access token for the ossrdbms-aad scope, handed to Npgsql through a periodic password provider (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:9-15).
    • Depends on: INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator, ConnectionStringSettings, DataSourcesSettings, AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings, AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource; externals Azure.Identity.DefaultAzureCredential and Npgsql NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder.UsePeriodicPasswordProvider.
    • Concept: token-as-password database auth, introduced on the AWS sibling RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator and mirrored for GCP by CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator; the Azure difference is that the token comes from DefaultAzureCredential. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses credential handling: no database password exists, and a connection string that does not require TLS is rejected because the token is a bearer credential sent as the password (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:13-15). [Rubric §14, Testability] assesses substitutable dependencies; an internal constructor takes the token source as a delegate.
    • Walkthrough: TokenRefreshInterval is four minutes (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:26) and FailureRetryInterval 30 seconds (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:29). The public constructor chains to new AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource(new DefaultAzureCredential()).GetTokenAsync (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35); the internal one accepts any Func<CancellationToken, Task<string>> for tests (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:41-42). Configure(builder) (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:46) checks TLS on the builder's connection string, naming its host (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:50-52), then installs UsePeriodicPasswordProvider(GetPasswordAsync, TokenRefreshInterval, FailureRetryInterval) (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:54). EnsureConfiguredConnectionStringsAreEncrypted(configuration) (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:65) collects the top-level ConnectionStrings:PostgreSQLConnectionString and every DataSources:{name}:PostgreSQLConnectionString (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:69-74) and checks each non-blank one parsed through NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder, the same parser Configure sees (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:76-80). GetPasswordAsync (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:87) honours cancellation and awaits the token source (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:89-92). EnsureEncrypted (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:95) accepts Require, VerifyCA or VerifyFull (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:97) and otherwise throws a message quoting AzurePostgreSql:EntraAuthentication:Enabled, the source and the fix (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:102-105).
    • Why it's built this way: the token lives 5 to 60 minutes, but the refresh interval is four minutes because the bound is the credential's cache: Azure.Identity hands out its cached token until fewer than five minutes remain, while Npgsql reuses what it was handed for the whole interval, so an interval under five minutes keeps every held token valid and each refresh is an in-memory read (AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:16-22). Checking configured connection strings at registration makes a cleartext misconfiguration fail at startup rather than on the first query (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:193-195), while Configure still catches one supplied later (ADR-070). Registration also adds a HostIntegrationMarker receipt the core startup tripwire checks, so setting the gate without the call fails the host (AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:197-205). See ADR-113 and ADR-134.
    • Where it's used: registered by AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication in AzureServiceCollectionExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:195,207); exercised by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: SQL Server, SQLite and Cosmos sources are untouched; the registration doc records that SQL Server takes Entra through the SqlClient extension, and that a connection string also carrying a static Password is refused by Npgsql when the data source is built (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:170-173). The database-side mapping of the Entra principal to a PostgreSQL role is provisioning, Not determinable from source.

    CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.CloudSql · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23 · Level 2 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: an INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator that puts every PostgreSQL data source on Cloud SQL IAM database authentication: the password becomes an OAuth2 access token for the sqlservice.login scope, handed to Npgsql through a periodic password provider (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:8-13).
    • Depends on: INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator, ConnectionStringSettings, DataSourcesSettings, CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings, CloudSqlAccessTokenSource; Npgsql NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder.UsePeriodicPasswordProvider.
    • Concept: token-as-password database auth, introduced on the AWS sibling RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator; the GCP difference is that the token is fetched from Google credentials rather than signed locally per host. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses credential handling: no database password exists, and because the token is a bearer credential sent as the password, a connection string that does not require TLS is rejected (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:11-13). [Rubric §14, Testability] assesses whether external dependencies can be substituted; an internal constructor takes the token source as a delegate.
    • Walkthrough: TokenRefreshInterval is one minute (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:26) and FailureRetryInterval 30 seconds (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:29). The public constructor chains to new CloudSqlAccessTokenSource().GetTokenAsync (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35); the internal one accepts any Func<CancellationToken, Task<string>> for tests (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:41-42). Configure(builder) (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:46) checks TLS on the builder's connection string, naming its host (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:50-52), then installs UsePeriodicPasswordProvider(GetPasswordAsync, TokenRefreshInterval, FailureRetryInterval) (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:54). EnsureConfiguredConnectionStringsAreEncrypted(configuration) (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:65) collects the top-level ConnectionStrings:PostgreSQLConnectionString and every DataSources:{name}:PostgreSQLConnectionString (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:69-74) and checks each non-blank one parsed through NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder, the same parser Configure sees (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:76-81). GetPasswordAsync (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:90) honours cancellation and awaits the token source (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:93-95); the token does not depend on the connection settings. EnsureEncrypted (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:98) accepts Require, VerifyCA or VerifyFull (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:100) and otherwise throws a message quoting CloudSql:IamAuthentication:Enabled, the source and the fix (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:105-108).
    • Why it's built this way: the token lives an hour, but the refresh interval is one minute because the bound is the credential's cache: Google's credential serves its cached token until fewer than 225 seconds remain and blocks for a new one only under 60, while Npgsql reuses what it was handed for the whole interval; a one-minute interval keeps every held token valid, and each refresh is an in-memory read (CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:15-21). Checking configured connection strings at registration makes a cleartext misconfiguration fail at startup rather than on the first query (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:101-103), while Configure still catches one supplied later (ADR-070). See ADR-061, ADR-113 and ADR-134.
    • Where it's used: registered by AddCloudSqlIamAuthentication in GcpServiceCollectionExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:103,116); exercised by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: SQL Server, SQLite and Cosmos sources are untouched; the registration doc comment records that Cloud SQL for SQL Server has no IAM database authentication and that behind the Cloud SQL Auth Proxy its --auto-iam-authn flag is used instead (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:79-84). The required roles (roles/cloudsql.instanceUser, roles/cloudsql.client, same comment, line 79) are not checked by code.

    GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:18 · Level 2 · class (internal static)

    • What it is: the two IDataProtectionBuilder extensions that wire the GCP key ring: PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage (repository) and ProtectKeysWithGoogleCloudKms (encryptor).
    • Depends on: GcsXmlRepository, GcsObjectLocation, GcpKmsXmlEncryptor, GcsStorageClientFactory, GcsFileStorageSettings; externals KeyManagementOptions, ConfigureOptions<T>, Google StorageClient, KeyManagementServiceClient, CryptoKeyName.
    • Concept: options-based plumbing of a DataProtection repository and encryptor. Each method registers an IConfigureOptions<KeyManagementOptions> whose factory resolves services lazily, so the client is chosen at options time rather than at registration. [Rubric §1, SOLID] assesses dependency inversion; here both collaborators are taken from the container when the host provided them.
    • Walkthrough: RetryDelay is 50 ms, the backoff unit handed to the repository (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:21). PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage(location) (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34) sets options.XmlRepository to a new GcsXmlRepository over the host's registered StorageClient, or else GcsStorageClientFactory.Create(new GcsFileStorageSettings()) (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:36-41). ProtectKeysWithGoogleCloudKms(keyName) (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:54) TryAddSingletons a default KeyManagementServiceClient.Create() (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:56) and sets options.XmlEncryptor to a GcpKmsXmlEncryptor over the container's client (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:57-59).
    • Why it's built this way: the fallback storage client is deliberately NOT added to the container, because a default registered here would win the TryAdd in a later AddGoogleCloudStorage and silently ignore its FileStorage:GcsServiceUrl (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:24-29). The KMS client, by contrast, must be in the container, because DataProtection activates GcpKmsXmlDecryptor per key and that type resolves the client there (GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:47-49). See ADR-069.
    • Where it's used: called only from GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:110,114), which calls the KMS step only when a Cloud KMS key name was configured and parsed (GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:93-99,112-115).

    AzureBlobFileStorageService

    MMCA.Common.Azure · MMCA.Common.Azure.Storage · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Storage/AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:20 · Level 4 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the Azure Blob Storage implementation of IFileStorageService. It uploads and deletes blobs inside the one container the host configured, and returns a Result rather than letting a storage or transport exception escape (AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:10-20).
    • Depends on: Azure.Storage.Blobs.BlobContainerClient and ILogger<T> as its two primary-constructor parameters (AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:20-22), IFileStorageService as the contract, FileUploadOptions for the extra headers, and Result / Error for its return shapes.
    • Concept introduced, taking the container as a dependency rather than creating it. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether an application holds only the permissions it needs, and [Rubric §17, DevOps] assesses whether resource provisioning lives with infrastructure rather than in application startup. The class doc is explicit (AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:11-13): the container, and its public-access level, is provisioned by infrastructure and not created here. A CreateIfNotExists in application code would need container-management rights at runtime. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] also applies: failures are logged with the exception and translated into a domain-shaped failure whose message says nothing about Azure, so the operator sees the cause and the caller sees a stable error code.
    • Walkthrough
      • IsConfigured is a constant true (AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:25). Its purpose is to differ from the false on NullFileStorageService.
      • UploadAsync(blobName, content, contentType, ct) (:29-30) forwards to the options overload with FileUploadOptions.None. Both overloads carry an RS0026 suppression (:28, :33): the pair is grandfathered on the interface, and the justification records that the type moved here from MMCA.Common.Infrastructure, so both overloads entered this package's public API as a pair.
      • The real work is UploadAsync(blobName, content, contentType, options, ct) (:34-72). It null-guards options (:36), resolves a blob client (:40), and uploads with BlobUploadOptions carrying the content type plus options.ContentDisposition and options.CacheControl as HTTP headers (:41-52), returning the blob's URI on success (:54).
      • Two catches yield the same Error.Failure with code FileStorage.UploadFailed: RequestFailedException, the Azure SDK's service failure (:56-63), and any exception IsTransportFailure accepts (:64-71).
      • IsTransportFailure (:107-109) accepts HttpRequestException, IOException, or an OperationCanceledException the caller did not request (a timeout after the client's own retries, :101-105). The caller's own cancellation is excluded and keeps propagating.
      • DeleteAsync(blobName, ct) (:75-99) uses DeleteBlobIfExistsAsync (:79), so an unknown blob name is success, which is what the interface promises ("unknown names succeed (idempotent)", MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Storage/IFileStorageService.cs:43). The same two catches yield FileStorage.DeleteFailed (:83-98).
    • Why it's built this way: ADR-045 records blob storage as the home for binary content the databases should not hold, and ADR-123 extends it to speaker and session assets. The registration is where the credential story lives (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:44-74): an absolute ServiceUri with DefaultAzureCredential is the managed-identity production path, ConnectionString is the local Azurite path, and ContainerName is required for either (:49-66). An incomplete section returns before registering anything, which leaves the null default in place, so a host can call AddAzureBlobFileStorage unconditionally.
    • Where it's used: registered by AddAzureBlobFileStorage (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:71), which MMCA.ADC's Identity and Conference service hosts call (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:261, MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:367). Consumers depend on IFileStorageService only. The S3 and GCS siblings are S3FileStorageService and GcsFileStorageService. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Storage/AzureBlobFileStorageServiceTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: whether the configured container is public-read or served through a signed URL is a provisioning decision, Not determinable from source.

    FcmInstallation

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FcmInstallation.cs:13 · Level 0 · record (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the in-memory shape of one Firestore installation document: InstallationId, UserId, Platform, PushChannel (FcmInstallation.cs:13-17), where PushChannel is the FCM registration token.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party beyond the UserIdentifierType alias (see the identifier-type aliases in the primer); System.Globalization.
    • Concept: cross-reference FirestoreInstallationRegistry, which explains why FCM needs a side registry at all (an FCM token carries no owner). It is the GCP twin of SnsInstallation, minus the SNS endpoint and subscription ARNs: FCM addresses the token directly and topic membership lives in FCM, not in the row.
    • Walkthrough: a positional record with no nullable members (FcmInstallation.cs:13-17). UserKey(userId) (FcmInstallation.cs:22-23) renders a user id as an invariant-culture string with string.Create(CultureInfo.InvariantCulture, ...); that string is what the UserId field stores and what queries filter on, so every writer and reader goes through this one method.
    • Where it's used: materialized by FirestoreInstallationRegistry.FromSnapshot (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FirestoreInstallationRegistry.cs:109-113), built by FcmPushDeviceRegistrar (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FcmPushDeviceRegistrar.cs:112), grouped by token in FcmNativePushSender (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FcmNativePushSender.cs:43-45), and the element type of every IFcmInstallationRegistry member.

    FcmNativePushSettings

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FcmNativePushSettings.cs:10 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the bound options for Firebase Cloud Messaging push, section NativePush:Fcm (FcmNativePushSettings.cs:13), gated by the shared sibling key NativePush:Enabled (FcmNativePushSettings.cs:16).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: cross-reference GcpServiceCollectionExtensions (AddFirebaseCloudMessagingPush) for the gate-plus-completeness registration pattern this class feeds; the AWS sibling is SnsNativePushSettings. [Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality] assesses whether configuration is explicit and safe by default; here the pipeline ships inert and is switched on by configuration alone, because the Firebase project and the Firestore database are provisioned outside the application (FcmNativePushSettings.cs:6-8).
    • Walkthrough: ProjectId (FcmNativePushSettings.cs:22) is the Google Cloud project that owns both the Firebase app (the FCM HTTP v1 sender) and the Firestore database holding the installation registry (FcmNativePushSettings.cs:18-21); InstallationsCollection (FcmNativePushSettings.cs:25) is the Firestore collection, one document per installation. IsComplete (FcmNativePushSettings.cs:28-30) requires both to be non-blank. There is no topic setting: delivery is per registration token only.
    • Why it's built this way: unlike SNS there is no credential or endpoint setting; the registration doc comment states credentials come from Application Default Credentials only, so there is no key-file setting (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:133-135). A gate that is off, or a section missing either value, makes the registration a no-op, so hosts call it unconditionally (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:130-133). See ADR-044.
    • Where it's used: AddFirebaseCloudMessagingPush reads the gate and section, returns early unless the gate parses true and IsComplete holds, then binds the options (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:153-162); FcmPushClientFactory takes it directly; FirestoreInstallationRegistry reads it through IOptions<FcmNativePushSettings>. Covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/Notifications/AddFirebaseCloudMessagingPushTests.cs.

    FcmSendOutcome

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FcmSendOutcome.cs:12 · Level 0 · record (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the result of one message in an FCM batch send: IsSuccess, an optional FirebaseAdmin MessagingErrorCode, and the optional exception (FcmSendOutcome.cs:12).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; FirebaseAdmin MessagingErrorCode.
    • Concept: a vendor-neutral-to-this-package outcome type, so the sender can be tested against a fake IFcmMessagingClient without constructing FirebaseAdmin's own batch response. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses whether a failure is classified before it is acted on; here only a failure that indicts the token prunes data.
    • Walkthrough: Success (FcmSendOutcome.cs:15) is the shared success instance. IsStaleToken (FcmSendOutcome.cs:25-28) is true for MessagingErrorCode.Unregistered, or for InvalidArgument only when the exception message contains "registration token" (ordinal, case-insensitive).
    • Why it's built this way: the doc comment records the asymmetry (FcmSendOutcome.cs:17-24): FCM answers a malformed token with InvalidArgument and a message naming the registration token, but any other InvalidArgument is about the message itself, would fail every token alike, and must never prune them. See ADR-044.
    • Where it's used: produced by FirebaseMessagingClient (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FirebaseMessagingClient.cs:31-33) and by the test doubles FakeFcmMessagingClient and RecordingFcmMessagingClient; consumed by FcmNativePushSender (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FcmNativePushSender.cs:66-79).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the stale-token classification depends on FCM's English error text; whether that wording is stable is not determinable from source.

    FirstFailure

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.Notifications · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FcmNativePushSender.cs:145 · Level 0 · class (private sealed, nested in FcmNativePushSender)

    • What it is: a tiny accumulator that keeps the first exception offered during one user-targeted send, which becomes the FirstFailure of the returned NativePushOutcome (FcmNativePushSender.cs:144).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: first-failure capture across a best-effort fan-out. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses whether partial failure is reported rather than swallowed; here the send keeps going past a failed lookup, chunk or token, yet the caller still learns that something failed and sees one concrete exception.
    • Walkthrough: Value (FcmNativePushSender.cs:147) has a private setter; Offer(failure) (FcmNativePushSender.cs:149) assigns with ??=, so the first non-null exception sticks and later offers (including null) are ignored.
    • Why it's built this way: the failure points are spread over two methods (the lookup in LookupAsync and the send loop in SendToUsersAsync), so one instance is created per send and passed into LookupAsync (FcmNativePushSender.cs:40,91). A dead token counts as a failure, since its device was not reached (FcmNativePushSender.cs:21-23). See ADR-044.
    • Where it's used: created once per SendToUsersAsync call (FcmNativePushSender.cs:39) and offered a failed user lookup (FcmNativePushSender.cs:103), a failed chunk (FcmNativePushSender.cs:59), a stale token (FcmNativePushSender.cs:72) and any other failed token (FcmNativePushSender.cs:77); its Value is returned as the outcome's FirstFailure (FcmNativePushSender.cs:87).

    GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.Secrets · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Secrets/GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:33 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the ConfigurationProvider that loads every Google Secret Manager secret under one id prefix into IConfiguration, mapping adc-prod-ConnectionStrings--Default to ConnectionStrings:Default (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:12-13), with an optional timed reload.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; Google SecretManagerServiceClient, Grpc.Core.RpcException, Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration.ConfigurationProvider.
    • Concept: the GCP sibling of AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationProvider and of the Key Vault provider used by AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions: one naming convention across clouds, so a secret set moves by renaming its prefix. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether secrets stay out of files and images and whether access is least-privilege; here values come from the vault at startup and each failure message names the exact IAM permission and role to grant. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] applies to the reload: a failed refresh keeps the last good values.
    • Walkthrough: constants KeyDelimiter = "--" and LatestVersion = "latest" (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:35-36); state is the client, project, prefix, optional interval, a disposal CancellationTokenSource, a Lock timer gate, the timer and a disposed flag (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:38-45). The internal constructor null-checks client, project and prefix (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:52-62). KeyFor(secretId, namePrefix) (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:74-82) returns null unless the id starts with the prefix ordinally and is longer than it, then strips the prefix and replaces -- with the configuration separator. Load() (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:85-99) sets Data = Read() and, with an interval, creates a one-shot Timer once under the gate (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:89-98). ReloadOnce (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:119-147) re-reads, swaps Data and calls OnReload() only when HasSameData is false (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:125-129), records any non-cancellation exception in LastReloadFailure (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:65,131-136), and re-arms the timer in finally unless disposed (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:139-145). Read() (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:149-186) lists secrets with the filter name:"{prefix}" (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:156), keeps only ids KeyFor accepts (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:164), wraps a listing RpcException in an InvalidOperationException naming secretmanager.secrets.list (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:170-175), then reads each value into a case-insensitive dictionary. ReadLatest (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:188-218) gets the latest version, returns null on NotFound (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:197-201) or a non-Enabled state (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:203-206), and otherwise accesses the concrete version name the state check saw, decoding the payload as UTF-8 (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:210). Dispose() (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:102-117) stops the timer under the gate and cancels in-flight calls.
    • Why it's built this way: the service's name filter is a case-insensitive substring match, so the ordinal prefix re-check keeps another environment's secrets out (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:17-19). Accessing the concrete version rather than the latest alias again means a rotation landing between the two calls cannot return a value whose state was never checked (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:208-209). Every call is synchronous because ConfigurationProvider.Load is synchronous and the host needs the values before the services that read them are registered; the one-shot re-armed timer means two loads never overlap (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:24-30). A throttled call or revoked grant during a reload must not take down a host that already has its values (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.cs:133-134). See ADR-061 and ADR-134.
    • Where it's used: built only by GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationSource (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Secrets/GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationSource.cs:31-32); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/Secrets/GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProviderTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: LastReloadFailure is internal and nothing in the defining file surfaces it to logs or health checks; whether any caller reads it outside tests is not shown in this brief.

    GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationSource

    MMCA.Common.Gcp · MMCA.Common.Gcp.Secrets · MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Secrets/GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationSource.cs:15 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the IConfigurationSource that carries a client factory, the project id, the name prefix and the reload interval, and builds a GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider.
    • Depends on: GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationProvider; Google SecretManagerServiceClient, IConfigurationSource.
    • Concept: the standard configuration source/provider split; cross-reference AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationSource for the identical AWS shape.
    • Walkthrough: primary-constructor parameters clientFactory, projectId, namePrefix, reloadInterval (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationSource.cs:15-19), with ProjectId, NamePrefix and ReloadInterval exposed as internal read-only properties (GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationSource.cs:22,25,28).
